## H — Embedded hardware & flight software

*Added 2026-09-18. This block owns what the PS asks for twice over and no criterion covered: requirement **R4** — *"embedded electronics + software for real-time flight control"* — and the **"Electronics block diagram"** deliverable (`notes/02-PGK-SIH-design-brief.md` §1). Risk 19 said it in as many words: *"no criterion in the register owns the flight computer (throughput, memory, timing margin)"*. Four rows that live elsewhere are closed **here**, at the row where their content actually sits: **N15** (time synchronisation, at H6), **W8** (bus voltages and rails, at H12), **R3** (BIT coverage, at H13) and **R4**'s response set (also at H13). Nothing in this block introduces new physics — every value is the hardware and software realisation of decisions already taken in G, C, N, W and F.*

| ID | Criterion | Type | Value | Basis / source | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| H1 | Flight computer architecture — the processor split and part class | Chosen | **Claimed — a dual-core processor with the two tasks split across cores, and no programmable logic in the kit.** The **Cortex-M7** core (480 MHz, double-precision FPU, 192 KB TCM) runs the navigation filter and the impact-point-prediction guidance law [N14, G1, G10]; the **Cortex-M4F** core (240 MHz) runs the **1 kHz control loop** — cascade, DOB, state-dependent limits and clamping — with its sampling and actuation instants set by hardware [G13, G12, G15]. Baseline part class: **STM32H745/755** (2 MB flash, 1 MB RAM — 192 KB M7 TCM + up to 864 KB shared SRAM; on-chip SMPS; extended ambient range to 125 °C; 14×14 mm TFBGA240 or LQFP); the exact part, package and temperature grade are a **P4 selection** against W9/W10 and E5/E6, with the H755's crypto/hash engine **selected at H11 (2026-09-19)** — the F12 package carries keys and they authenticate the GNSS signal. **This amends G13's *mechanism*, not its content.** G13's content is that control timing is independent of the propagation load; the fabric was its means. Three properties carry the content onto cores: **(1) two cores, two NVICs, independent clock dividers — no *software* path exists by which the propagation load can delay the loop**, which is precisely what G13's rejected option B (a single processor on a shared timebase) would have created; **(2) the sampling instants stay hardware-timed** — HRTIM (≈2 ns class) triggers the ADCs, DMA collects them, so *when* the loop samples carries no software jitter at all, and the core's only obligation is to finish computing before the next PWM update: a **bounded-WCET** obligation of a few microseconds against a 1 000 µs period (≈1 % duty, ≈100× margin, so a 10× pessimism still leaves 10×); **(3) the protection path is hardware** — comparator and desaturation trips into an HRTIM fault input shut the gate drive down without software. **What is genuinely weaker, stated rather than glossed:** the fabric was a *hardware* guarantee and would have survived a fault in the application domain; the two cores share a PLL, a power domain and the bus matrix, so the guarantee is now an **argument plus a measurement** — a WCET budget (H4), a **memory-partitioning rule** (the loop's code and data confined to a RAM partition the guidance core's working set never touches — H2) and a jitter measurement on the R7 rig. **Fallback priced, not hidden:** a flash-based FPGA can take the loop at P4 without moving the software boundary. **Consequences carried:** the M7's double-precision FPU is available to the navigation and guidance chain, with the precision actually used (single or double, per term) fixed by H4's budget rather than assumed here; W4's existing ≈5 W line for "flight processor + FPGA" keeps margin it no longer needs; mass and volume fall against risk 33 and risk 27 | H1 decided 2026-09-18 — **the choice is the assistant's recommendation, made on the user's instruction to decide ("whichever you recommend"); it is not a user technical instruction.** Amends G13's mechanism in the same pass and logs risk 36. Supersedes the assistant's earlier option (a Zynq-class processor + fabric SoC — the reference bench's own silicon class), withdrawn on toolchain, mass and production-sourcing grounds at equal functional fit. Part facts public (ST datasheet / AN5557). Feeds H2–H5 | DEFINED — **assistant's recommendation on the user's instruction to decide**; part and package are P4 selections |
| H2 | Flight-software architecture — task map, inter-core contract, and the ground / flight builds | Chosen | **Claimed — a tool-agnostic, single-source architecture: the portable C core is the only implementation of anything that flies, and the design bench is a *client* of it, never a second implementation.** **(1) The core.** Control loop (cascade, DOB, live-limit clamping), nav filter and guidance/IPP written once in portable C behind fixed interfaces, plus a thin HAL. One source tree, three builds: the **flight image** (M7/M4F); a **rig/HIL test binary** (the R8 deliverable); and a **native library** the R5 campaign harness and the agent's CI link against — so "loop in the loop" is literal: the ≈10,000-flight campaign runs the files the flight image is built from. **(2) The design bench — tool-agnostic by user decision 2026-09-19:** GNU Octave now (installed and MCP-wired), **Simulink if a licence appears**. It owns control design, linearisation, margins and the DOB discretisation, and it *runs the actual core* in the loop — Simulink via a Legacy Code Tool S-function (no code-generation licence needed), Octave via `mkoctfile --mex`. Its outputs are designs and **golden vectors**, never a flight algorithm. **(3) The parity gate.** Golden vectors generated on the bench are committed to the repo; CI re-runs them against the core on every change with no MATLAB/Octave required; a change is real only when it is in the core and passes the vectors. **The fork not taken: Simulink Coder-generated flight C** — held as a fallback only (it would put a MATLAB licence in the flight build's lifecycle and hand the agent a non-editable artefact). **Task map (derived):** **M4F** — the 1 kHz cascade + DOB + limits, sampling and actuation instants hardware-timed (HRTIM → ADC → DMA), no OS; its code and data are a **dedicated partition in D2-domain SRAM (SRAM1–3)** — *not* the M7's TCM, which the CM4 cannot address at all — entered from its own flash bank and relocated to that partition before the mission so loop fetch carries no ART/cache variability. **M7** — sensor ingest (ring IMU, body-side spin channel, GNSS, magnetometer), nav filter, one IPP propagation per ~0.17 s (≈6 Hz design point, ≤10 Hz ceiling), mode logic, boot/setter path (F11/F12), the one-way fuze correction link (F1), R10's log. **Inter-core contract (derived):** a fixed-size mailbox in the shared SRAM regions the RM reserves for inter-core use (SRAM3/SRAM4), taken and released with the **HSEM hardware semaphores** — raw, not OpenAMP/RPMsg, because the message set is tiny and fixed — with the M7 cleaning the mailbox's cache lines before each release, and a watchdog on both sides. **M4F owns actuation and never depends on the M7 for loop closure:** a stalled guidance core leaves the ring held at its last *relative* setpoint, so the steering-force direction rotates with the body and largely averages out — a bounded perturbation, G11-safe, with R4's "no mode worse than unguided" assessment owed when R4 lands | User decision 2026-09-19 (option 3 of three: tool-agnostic — Octave now, Simulink later if licensed, the golden vectors the constant). Task map and inter-core contract derived in the same pass from H1, G8–G13, G15, F1, F11/F12 and R10. Part facts: STM32H745 datasheet and RM0399 (TCM is CM7-exclusive; HSEM; SRAM3/4 inter-core regions) | DEFINED — the build strategy is the user's decision; the task map and contract are derived in this row and override-able |
| H3 | Memory architecture and data retention | Derived | **DERIVED — the part is not the constraint; the *log* is the one item that can exceed it, and bounding it is R10's.** Against the STM32H745 class (**2 MB flash, two banks; 1 MB RAM = 192 KB TCM, CM7-exclusive — 64 ITCM + 128 DTCM, invisible to the CM4 — plus 864 KB user SRAM** across D1/D2/D3): **(1) Program** — M7 image (nav filter, IPP, mode logic, drivers, logging) ≈ 150–300 KB and M4F image (loop, DOB, limits, ADC/PWM) ≈ 30–50 KB, both far inside 2 MB; the M4 image relocates its hot code into the ≈ 32 KB D2 partition (H2). **(2) M7 working set** — nav filter at ≈ 16–20 states → 20² covariance ≈ 3.2 KB plus Jacobians, noise and scratch ≈ 40–60 KB; IPP propagation scratch ≈ 10–20 KB; sensor and log staging ≈ 30–60 KB → **≈ 100–150 KB, met by the 512 KB AXI SRAM.** **(3) Coefficient tables** — the aero set at a 20 Mach × 9 α × 9 β grid × 8 coefficients × 8 B ≈ **104 KB of flash**, read-only and cached; the grid resolution is ours to choose and the budget affords a generous one. **(4) Mission package** — target, met, charge/velocity, time reference, GNSS ephemeris and keys, mode and parameters, round ID ≈ **≤ 16 KB**, written by the setter to **non-volatile** storage; the derived requirement is **retention across power loss from the gun line to launch**, not merely storage. **(5) The log — the finding.** Full-flight raw telemetry does not fit: at 100 Hz × ≈ 100 B over the ≈ 90 s flight it is ≈ **0.9 MB** (half the flash), and at IMU rate (≈ 1 kHz) ≈ **8–9 MB — over four times the on-chip flash.** So R10 must choose between **(a)** selective or downsampled logging inside the on-chip flash (rides the 2 MB, no new part, costs evidence depth), **(b)** a dedicated serial-NOR part (8–64 MB), priced against risk 27 (nose volume), risk 33 (mass) and K5 (cost), or **(c)** full-rate windows only — launch, apogee, terminal. **Carried to R10 as a constraint, not an assumption.** **(6) Retention rule** — anything that must outlive power (mission package, log) lives in flash with committed writes; RAM is not assumed to survive, since W5's reserve battery holds the safe state and boot, not the flight computer | Derived from H1 (part class), H2 (partition), N14/N8 (filter and IMU rate — provisional), F11/F12 (setter package), R10 (log scope) and the aero set (risk 8); part facts from the STM32H745 datasheet and RM0399 | DERIVED — provisional (N14, R10, F11, risk 8); **derivation confirmed by the user 2026-09-19** |
| H4 | Compute and timing budget — the IPP deadline and the 1 ms cycle | Derived | **DERIVED — both deadlines carry ≈10× margin at the design point; with H1 and H3 this row closes risk 19.** **(1) M4F, the 1 kHz loop.** Per cycle: the cascade (P over PI), the DOB (Q filter and Ĝ⁻¹ scaling), both limit routes and the clamping test, ADC/DMA service and the PWM update — order **200–500 arithmetic operations**, single precision (the M4F's FPU is single-precision only), no OS, code and data in the D2 partition, sampling instants from HRTIM. **Budget: WCET ≤ 100 µs (10 % of the 1 000 µs period); design estimate ≈ 5–15 µs (0.5–1.5 %); measured on the target at P4, jitter on R7** — so a 10× pessimism on the estimate still sits inside the budget. This is the number risk 36(b) carries. **(2) M7, the guidance propagation.** One IPP propagation per interval: ≈ 86 s of flight integrated at ≈ 0.1 s steps with four evaluations per step ≈ **1–2 MFLOPs**, aero lookups included, in double precision on the M7's DP FPU. **Budget ≤ 100 ms against G13's ≈ 0.17 s interval; design estimate ≈ 5–20 ms (3–12 %)**; ≈ 300 propagations per flight (G10) ≈ 2–6 s of CPU over the 90 s flight (2–7 % average). **Overrun degrades gracefully** — the law skips to the next interval rather than disturbing the loop, which is the property H1 bought. **(3) M7, the nav filter.** EKF at 16–20 states: the covariance update is O(N³) ≈ 10⁴ FLOPs per step, so at N8's ≥ 100 Hz (200 Hz design) ≈ 2–5 MFLOP/s — ≈ 1 % of the M7. Not a constraint. **(4) Precision, fixed per block here** (H2's parity gate): **loop single precision; nav and guidance double.** The bench-vs-core equivalence tolerance is stated per block — compared signal-by-signal over a scenario at **≤ 1e-4 relative RMS for the single-precision blocks, ≤ 1e-9 for the double blocks** — with the **PIL run on the target as the authority**, since host and target float paths are never bit-identical. Recorded because the reference's fixed-point loop showed quantisation surfacing as a residual limit cycle: float avoids re-creating it, and this row fixes the precision so it cannot drift | Derived from H1 (part class, core split, WCET obligation), H2 (task map), G13 (1 kHz; one propagation per ≈ 0.17 s), G10 (≈ 300 propagations per flight; the rate ceiling that bounds the guidance update), G8/G9 (the bandwidths the loop must realise), G15/G12 (the per-cycle work), N8 (≥ 100 Hz IMU — provisional) and the integration scheme (ours to choose — 0.1 s steps, four evaluations per step assumed) | DERIVED — provisional (integration scheme and step size, the aero set's size, N14's filter order); **measurement carried at P4 (WCET) and R7 (jitter)** |
| H5 | Board partition, stack and packaging — launch survival | Chosen | **Claimed — one annular stack in the well spigot, carried by a spine (option A): the guidance kit's boards live as annular layers about F9's reserved train bore, in the Ø30–Ø48 mm annulus of the 124.7 mm intrusion, and take no nose volume.** Board set: flight computer (M7/M4F, H1), power stage (machine and dump drives, H8/H9), sensor conditioning (H6), the supercapacitor buffer (W4) as distributed ring cells, the log store (R10) and the rail distribution (H12). **Launch survival is a *load-path* requirement, not a board-strength one:** a 20 g board at 15 000 g is 294 N — trivial alone — so the design rule is C35's: **the spine carries the machine's reaction into the well shoulder and nothing compliant sits in that path**; the boards and their connectors hang off the spine and are never load-bearing. **Thermal:** the stack sits against the well wall — the very surface W7/P6 specify as the dump's interface — so board heat and dump heat share one route into the shell, and the stack's bond to the wall enters P6's conductance budget. **Form (derived):** usable area is ≈ 11 cm² per annular layer (`π/4·(48² − 30²)`), so the ≈ 60–100 cm² the electronics need is **6–10 layers** — length is not the constraint; **segmented flat boards on a carrier** (standard PCB outlines, cheaper at K5's volume, testable out of the stack) are preferred to custom ring outlines, with ring boards the fallback if area or stiffness proves short. **Assembly:** axial — layers slide onto the spine and are clamped; the harness runs forward through the spine to the machine, the ring and the fuze. **Costs accepted:** the annular form carries a K5 premium and components must be low-profile; the gate-drive harness crosses the machine section. **The annulus is shared:** the fuze's setter coupling (F11) and reserve battery (W5) want the same volume, so the division between the two electronics sets is **H10's**, and risk 27 is extended to cover it. **Contingency priced, not hidden:** the gate-drive stage alone can move forward beside the machine — option B's one advantage — if EMC testing shows it necessary; a change, not a redesign | User decision 2026-09-19 (option A of three: A single spigot stack, B two zones split forward/aft, C all forward around the machine). Constraints: F9 (reserved train bore), C35 (no compliant material in the launch path), risk 27 (nose volume), P9/risk 33 (mass), P7/P12 (the Ø50.8 mm well stack), W7/P6 (well wall as thermal interface) | DEFINED — the user's decision; board form, layer count and retention are P4 (CAD) outputs |
| H6 | Sensor acquisition chain and time base | Chosen / Derived | **Claimed — one hardware time base with GNSS as an *epoch label*, never the clock source (option A), and acquisition split by consumer: loop-side on the M4F, mission-side on the M7.** **(1) The time base.** A shared 32-bit hardware counter in the D2 domain, readable by both cores, is the kit's clock; the loop's HRTIM is locked to the same clock source and its residual phase against the counter is measured once at startup — H1's "independent clock dividers" caveat discharged by a one-time measurement rather than by software. **(2) The acquisition split.** *M4F, hardware-triggered (H1/H4):* the ring pickoff N10 — the loop's own feedback (G5) — plus phase currents and bus voltage (H8) and the machine's monitoring, all stamped at conversion by the ADC/DMA path. *M7:* the ring IMU through the H7 link (≥ 100 Hz, N8), GNSS (N2), the magnetometer (N12), the barometer (N11) and the machine's slow monitoring, each stamped at conversion, never in software. **(3) The spin channel is one sensor with two consumers** — G15's capability route reads available current against measured relative speed, F15's notch locks to the same 7× rotation, and the nav filter needs it for attitude: **N10 is read loop-side by necessity, and ω_rel crosses to the M7 in the H2 mailbox**, which therefore carries this quantity by name along with the timestamp format. **(4) GNSS is a label, never a source:** PPS and the time message *measure* the counter's offset from UTC, and the solution carries its own measurement epoch so receiver latency is removed rather than modelled — the clock is never steered, which is what lets timing survive jamming and outages (N5, N4) where a disciplined clock would have made GNSS a *timing* dependency as well as a position one. **(5) N15 closes here:** the derived requirement is **timestamp uncertainty ≤ 100 µs (1σ) for the mission-side set** — comfortably inside N1's 1.0 m time/lever-arm term (≈ 1.1 ms at ≈ 900 m/s) and met by conversion-time stamping against a 5 ns-class counter with a TCXO reference. **The one place the rule needs care, handed to H7:** the ring IMU must timestamp **on the ring**, or the link's delay must be measured and bounded — the rotating joint is the only place where "timestamp at conversion" cannot be taken for granted | User decision 2026-09-19 (option A of three: A hardware base with GNSS as label, B GNSS-disciplined clock, C software timestamps with filter-estimated delays). Realises N15 and serves N2/N3, N7/N8, N9–N13; feeds H2's mailbox content, H7's link payload, H8's current sensing and H13's BIT. Rejected: B makes GNSS a timing single point (N4/N5); C puts load-dependent jitter into the navigation innovation | DEFINED — the user's decision; **N15 closes with it** |
| H7 | Ring-side electronics and the contactless power / data link | Chosen | **Claimed — a rotary transformer for power and an *optical* link for data (option A), with H6's timestamp obligation discharged by the optical path's bounded, measurable delay.** **Power:** a rotary transformer across the joint, concentric with the bearing and inside C22's envelope and risk 27's volume — the ring only *receives*, because all flight power is generated body-side (C34's coils are on the stator), so it carries the ring-side load: the IMU, its conditioning and the link's ring end, **≈ 0.5–1.5 W**, with the transformer's own losses landing in W4's budget as a small addition. **Data:** an LED/photodiode pair across the gap — chosen because the channel is immune to the alternator's fields, which matters twice in this volume: F15's coexistence is already fought here, and a *magnetic* data channel would add a source immediately beside N12's magnetometer. One channel carries the IMU's data up; a second narrow channel down is reserved for configuration and the timestamp marker. **Timing (H6's obligation, discharged):** the optical propagation delay is negligible and the emitter/detector latency is fixed and measurable, so the IMU's samples are stamped against the H6 time base with a *measured, bounded correction* — **no ring-side clock is required**, which is the concrete reason optics beat a transformer-coupled frame here. **Stay-out:** N10's pickoff (ring angle and spin) remains body-side, so the link carries **data only** — a link fault degrades the navigation, never the 1 kHz loop's feedback. **Fit and mass:** the ring-side assembly (IMU + conditioning + link end + transformer secondary) must fit the ring's annulus inside C22's envelope and risk 27's volume, and its mass counts against C26's 0.5 kg ring assumption and risk 33 — both P4 CAD outputs. **Verification:** the R7 rig runs exactly this joint | User decision 2026-09-19 (option A of three: A optical, B capacitive, C inductive). Constraints: N6's ring mount, N10, C22, C13 (contactless — a slip ring would import friction into the feasibility-gated budget), F15/N12 (magnetic coexistence in the same volume), H6 (timestamping) and **risk 34, which this row owns** | DEFINED — the user's decision; ring-side fit, mass, and the optical alignment across C35's 0.20 mm deflection budget are P4 (CAD) outputs |
| H8 | Machine power electronics — rectification, motoring, sensing and gate drives | Chosen | **Claimed — keep the confirmed winding and set the bus *below the flight-minimum EMF* (option A, with W8/C17): the stage is a buck in generation and a boost in motoring for the whole flight, with no buck↔boost mode crossing at any instant.** The finding it settles: `E = Kv·ω_rel` falls with spin — **400 V at launch to ≈ 230 V late** — so **any bus inside that band is crossed once per flight, exactly at the mode boundary**, and at the 400 V bus as previously chosen that crossing sat at the launch condition, the point of peak load (2.92 kW, C42). Putting the bus below `E_min` removes the crossing entirely. **Content:** a machine-side four-quadrant stage (buck in generation, boost in motoring, synchronous rectification) with **its current loop closed here** — so G15's limit law commands a *converter current setpoint*, fast and precise, rather than a load-bank resistance; current and voltage sensing for G15 (coil current, relative speed via N10) and F15's current-proportional calibration; gate drives with comparator and desaturation trips into HRTIM's fault input (H1); switching spectrum per F15 (≥ 100 kHz, controlled edge rate). **Bus value ≈ 150–200 V** — set against the dump's current and the buffer's ESR, formally closing with the rail set at H12 (W8). **Costs accepted:** bus-side current doubles at the peak — ≈ 16.7 A at 175 V against 7.3 A at 400 V — carried by the dump (H9/W12), the bus wiring and the low-voltage leg; **machine-side currents are unchanged**, the machine's insulation sees only its own EMF (≤ 400 V), so C14–C19 stand exactly as confirmed and C17's turns count is not re-cut | User decision 2026-09-19 (option A of three: A bus below the flight-minimum EMF, B re-cut the winding for EMF headroom at a 400 V bus, C leave both and handle the crossing at launch). Constraints: C14–C20, C41, W4's motoring duty, G15's limit laws, F15's calibration, G7's degeneracy finding | DEFINED — the user's decision; the bus value and the regulated rail set close at H12 (W8) |
| H9 | Dump stage drive and thermometry | Chosen / Derived | **Claimed — a chopper-driven shunt regulator whose *first* protection action is to derate the limit law, not to disconnect (option A).** The dump holds the H8 bus at its setpoint and burns the surplus — **2.92 kW at the peak, ≈ 121 kJ over the flight** (C42) — with the buffer covering the transients between generation and demand; its drive is a **chopper at ≥ 100 kHz with a controlled edge rate**, meeting F15's switching-spectrum requirement so it cannot corrupt the machine's own current measurement. **Thermometry** senses the element and the interface and yields a thermal-margin estimate, which enters **G15's limit law as a further limit**: as margin shrinks the protection *reduces the current limit* — trading guidance authority for thermal margin deliberately and visibly, through the same law that already schedules authority against available current — and only a continued fall triggers a **hard trip** as the last resort. **The guidance law is told** (H2's mailbox carries the limit multiplier), so G3's gate and the IPP spend the remaining authority properly rather than discovering the shortfall in flight. **Rating derived:** 2.92 kW at the H8 bus ≈ **16.7 A** (against 7.3 A at the superseded 400 V bus — the price H8 accepted); the element is W12's (0.15–0.30 kg, bonded to the stator mount) and its bond to the well wall is P6's interface budget. **Failure direction stated:** a trip is *worse* than a derate — it collapses the machine's current and presents G15 with a supply collapse it never commanded (risk 17's family) — which is why the derate is the first action and the trip the last | User decision 2026-09-19 (option A of three: A derate then trip, B hard cut-out, C no active protection). Constraints: C42, W7, W12, P6, G15, F15, W4's buffer, risk 17, risk 33 | DEFINED — the user's decision; the element's rating, thermometry siting and the trip threshold are P4 outputs |
| H10 | Fuze electronics partition and the guidance ↔ fuze interfaces | Chosen | **Claimed — two processors, one physical boundary (option A): the fuze keeps its own small MCU on its own board and its own power path, and the kit's computer supplies only the bounded, one-way time correction (and receives status).** The fuze-side set — arming logic, mode logic, the detector's signal processing, the timer, the S&A interlock and the setter read-back — runs on the fuze MCU in the **W5 reserve-battery domain**; the guidance-side set (nav, guidance, loop, logging, power electronics) stays on the H745/755. **F1's autonomy becomes a property rather than an argument:** the fuze arms and functions on its own, the one-way bounded and arming-gated correction is enforced by *wiring* rather than by protocol discipline, and a guidance-side fault leaves the fuze whole — its fallback is the pre-set time, which it holds itself. **The safety case stays separable:** the arming chain and the fuze's own function share no silicon or software with flight-critical guidance, which is what a MIL-STD-1316 reviewer asks for first. **Annullus accounting (H5/risk 27):** the fuze board is now a named claimant in the spigot annulus, and its footprint and the guidance stack divide H5's 6–10 layers between them. **Cost accepted:** one small MCU, a few cm² of board, a few grams and its own regulation — against risk 33 (mass) and K4 (cost), where a small MCU is the cheapest item in the kit. **The one-die alternative is refuted, not merely disfavoured:** putting the fuze's logic on the M4F alongside the 1 kHz loop reintroduces exactly the software coupling H1's timing guarantee (risk 36) exists to exclude. **Constraint handed to H11:** the setter must be able to program the fuze domain — including its pre-set time — **with the kit's computer unpowered**, or the fallback everything rests on inherits a dependency | User decision 2026-09-19 (option A of three: A two processors, B one computer with a protected fuze domain on the M7, C the fuze on the M4F alongside the loop). Constraints: F1's autonomy provision, F6/F7's arming interlock, F2/F3 (detector processing), F5 (delay), W5 (reserve domain), H5 (annulus), risk 26, risk 27, risk 36 | DEFINED — the user's decision; the fuze MCU's class and its environmental selection (E5/E6) are P4 outputs |
| H11 | Setter interface electronics | Chosen / Derived | **Claimed — the keys authenticate the *signal*, not only the setter (option A): authenticated GNSS with the H755's crypto/hash engine, and the inductive gun-line interface that loads and verifies the package.** **(1) Signal integrity.** The receiver's authentication service (OSNMA-class where the constellation offers it, confirmed with N2/F11) validates the navigation message; the H755's crypto/hash engine holds the keys and the nav filter fuses the integrity flag, rejecting measurements that fail authentication. This closes the one attack *worse* than jamming — a spoofed fix steers a **guided** round confidently to the wrong place, the same failure shape as risk 28 and equally invisible to the G4 budget (M5 measures against the loaded aimpoint) — and it resolves H1's deferral: **the H755 variant is selected, superseding the H745 baseline.** **(2) The setter path.** The physical front end (coil, rectifier, demodulator) sits in the **always-alive reserve-battery domain**, so its demodulated stream exists with the kit computer unpowered; **both domains listen to it independently and each validates its own read-back**, which discharges H10's constraint (the fuze's pre-set time is programmable without the kit's computer) while giving neither domain a data path through the other. F12's verified read-back, crew go/no-go and target sanity check are implemented guidance-side; the fuze's own settings are verified by the fuze. **(3) Cost and conditions.** The price is the H755 premium (K4/K5) and a key path (setter → kit → engine) — and **the capability is conditional**: the constellation's authentication service and the receiver's support are F11/N2 post-selection confirmation items, with the B position (setter authentication only, no anti-spoof claim) as the stated fallback — logged as **risk 39** so the conditionality is visible where the claims are | User decision 2026-09-19 (option A of three: A authenticate the signal with the H755 engine, B authenticate the setter only on H745, C no key use with spoofing out of scope). Constraints: F11 (in-service interface, no new gun-line equipment), F12 (package, read-back, sanity check), H10 (dual-domain programming), N2/N5 (constellation and anti-jam), K4/K5 | DEFINED — the user's decision; **H1's crypto deferral is closed (H755 selected)**; the service and receiver support are post-selection confirmation items (risk 39) |
| H12 | Power distribution, rails and isolation | Chosen / Derived | **Claimed — passive diode-OR hand-over, per-domain converters and an isolated boundary (option A); W8 closes here.** **(1) Hand-over.** The reserve domain (W5) feeds the fuze, its arming path and the setter front end pre-launch; as spin builds, the machine's bus rises above the reserve rail and takes over **through a diode-OR** — no active changeover to fail, and the failure direction is right: a fault takes the guidance domain and never the reserve/arming path. **(2) Rails (W8's closure).** From the machine bus — **set at 180 V nominal**, below the flight-minimum EMF of ≈ 230 V with the buck at D ≤ 0.78 at the worst point and ≈ 16.2 A at the 2.92 kW peak: the machine-side gate-drive rail and the dump chopper (H8/H9); the guidance domain's 3.3 V and core rails through its own converters; the ring's **≈ 0.5–1.5 W** rail through H7's rotary transformer; the buffer (W4) on the bus behind its bidirectional interface; the fuze domain's own rail off the reserve supply; and the setter front end on the always-alive rail. **(3) Isolation and returns.** The fuze domain keeps its own ground reference, and H10's one-way correction crosses by **optical isolation** — the same technology H7 buys — so the boundary is electrical as well as logical; single-point grounds per domain, star-pointed at each domain's own converter. **(4) What each domain does when it fails** (R4's rule stated where the power lives): the reserve/arming path has no dependence on the flight bus or the guidance domain; the guidance domain's loss leaves the fuze autonomous (F1) with its pre-set time; and nothing in the rail architecture can make a failure mode worse than unguided | User decision 2026-09-19 (option A of three: A passive diode-OR with per-domain converters, B active changeover, C single trickle-charged rail). Constraints: W4 (buffer on the bus), W5 (reserve domain), W6 (shelf life), **W8 (closes here)**, H8/H9 (bus loads), H10 (domain separation), H7 (ring power), F6/F7 (arming-path independence), R4 | DEFINED — the user's decision; **W8 closes with it**; converter loading and rail tolerances are P4 outputs |
| H13 | BIT architecture, coverage and fault response | Chosen / Derived | **Claimed — two-tier BIT with a graded response (option A): a full pre-launch go/no-go, and an in-flight BIT whose job is *classification*, not coverage.** **(1) Pre-launch — the crew's only defence.** The setter read-back (F12), the fuze's own self-test (timer, detector, S&A position, the arming interlock), the guidance POST (cores, memory, the ADC/DMA path, the machine's integrity, the rail set) and a sensor-plausibility pass; the action is the **crew abort**, so the detection requirement is high — the coverage target is stated and justified rather than implied, and **R3 closes with it**. **(2) In flight — classification, not coverage.** Continuous health monitoring — the H2 watchdogs, the loop's health against H4's WCET budget, sensor reasonableness, and the limit laws' own expectations (H8/H9) — whose output selects a **response**, not a score: **hold** (H2's last valid command), **derate** (H9's limit multiplier) or **fuze-autonomous** (F1), with everything logged (R10) as after-action evidence. **(3) R4's rule is the acceptance test:** every fault class must leave a mode **no worse than unguided** — which is why the ladder contains no shutdown (option C's failsafe would *break* the rule, not satisfy it) and why the reserve/arming path (W5, F6, H12) sits outside the guidance domain's fault set by construction. **Derived with it:** the fault-class → detection → response table with R4's verdict per class, the pre-launch checklist the crew actually reads, and the BIT's log fields feeding R10 | User decision 2026-09-19 (option A of three: A two-tier graded response, B pre-launch only, C in-flight with a hard failsafe). Closes **R3** and fixes **R4**'s response set; serves F12's go/no-go and F1's fuze autonomy; carries H2, H4, H8, H9 and the W5/F6/H12 separation | DEFINED — the user's decision; **R3 closes with it and R4's response set is fixed**; the per-class table is the verification artifact (P4/R5) |

## E — Environment & qualification

| ID | Criterion | Type | Value | Basis / source | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| E1 | ⚑ Axial acceleration: g-level, **pulse shape and duration** | Given | 15 000 g **[ref]** — **and the shape is now as important as the peak, with a method behind it.** The gun-hard MEMS literature specifies the launch shock as a **long-duration component (>5 ms) with a superimposed high-frequency component** against a 10 000–20 000 g peak, so a bare g-number is not a test condition and this row's "pulse shape and duration" clause is the load-bearing half. **Closure route now named (2026-09-19):** the established method is material characterisation at high strain rate (Split Hopkinson Bar and 4-point bend on the actual substrate) → explicit finite-element modelling → very-high-g test with a **potted accelerometer** to capture the imposed loading, run on a national facility with guns to 21″ at >2 000 ft/s (NSWCIH). This supplies both the criterion form — a **damage-tolerance criterion** for projectile-borne electronics under in-bore launch — and the protection answer the design already assumes: **potting is established practice, not our own guess** (H5). The same method backs E2 and E4, and R7's setback-representative pulse on the buffer subassembly (risk 35) | YIL / gun data for the level; **pulse shape and duration from the gun-hard MEMS IMU specification**; method from NSWCIH *High Shock Modeling of Fuze Components*, 64th NDIA Fuze Conference 2021; damage-tolerance criterion from Defence Technology 2026; MEMS survivability to 20 000 g (60 000 g by gas gun). Source register D1, D2, D3, D4, D5 | OPEN — **level `[ref]` pending YIL; the test method and failure criterion are now named rather than absent** |
| E2 | Radial acceleration | Given | 5 000 g **[ref]** | YIL / gun data | OPEN |
| E3 | Vibration: random and sinusoidal levels | Given | | MIL-STD-810 | OPEN |
| E4 | Shock: setback, set-forward, handling, drop | Given | | MIL-STD-810 | OPEN |
| E5 | Storage temperature range | Given | | MIL-STD-810 | OPEN |
| E6 | Operating temperature range | Given | | MIL-STD-810 | OPEN |
| E7 | Humidity, salt fog, sand and dust | Given | | MIL-STD-810 | OPEN |
| E8 | EMC standard | Given | MIL-STD-461 | | OPEN |
| E9 | Aerodynamic heating | Derived | | From W9 | OPEN |
| E10 | Qualification standards list | Given | MIL-STD-810, MIL-STD-461, MIL-STD-1316 | | OPEN |
| E11 | Insensitive munitions assessment | Given | AOP-39 / STANAG 4439 | | OPEN |
| E12 | **HERO / EMR / ESD — hazards of electromagnetic radiation to ordnance, electromagnetic radiation, and electrostatic discharge** | Given | **Required — the kit's electro-explosive devices and its electronics shall meet the applicable ordnance electromagnetic-hazard and ESD requirements, and the standard set shall be named rather than implied.** The gap: E10's qualification list and E11's IM assessment do not cover ordnance electromagnetic hazards, and this kit has three exposures that the fielded fuze it replaces also has plus one it does not — a fireset with an out-of-line rotor (F6), a **24 GHz emitter in the nose** (F2), and a machine whose magnets and switching converter are a designed-in magnetic source (F15). **The parallel with F6 is exact and worth stating in the submission:** F6 says the fuze cannot *initiate* without two independent environment stimuli; E12 says it cannot be *made* to initiate by an external field. Together they are the two halves of the "nothing functions until we say so, and nothing prevents it from functioning when we say so" argument. **Standard set to be fixed at P4** — MIL-HDBK-240 / MIL-STD-464 class for HERO and EME, STANAG 4234 class for EMR, MIL-STD-1686 / STANAG 4497 class for ESD — editions verified against YIL's qualification baseline post-selection. Note that HERO assessment is a *system* property (round plus platform plus magazine), so this row is owned jointly with P12's well stack and the F6 arming logic | Standard ordnance electromagnetic-hazard and ESD practice (MIL-HDBK-240 / MIL-STD-464 / STANAG 4234 / MIL-STD-1686 class); identified as absent from the qualification set by the verified-source audit §3. Source register F3, A2 | OPEN — standards named at P4, qualification verified at P7; **risk 42** |

Feeds K5, X16 (the seeker delta is measured against this baseline), risk 30 | DEFINED 2026-09-18 — **the target is a published claim; K5 is the evidence** |

Read with the volume difference in view, its value is directional rather than numerical: K4's ₹6.5 lakh is a *price* target anchored to the fielded product, so a comparable bill of materials an order of magnitude below it is the expected shape. **The exposure is therefore not whether the BOM closes** — it is that K5 must show the **margin structure** (qualification, tooling, test, programme) that separates a price from a BOM, and must rank the drivers **at 5,000/yr**, not at that study's 1,000/yr | Starting list and anchor: the predecessor study's BOM (different architecture, same class, 1,000/yr — **in-house data, not a public source**); drivers from C22, C40, C41, C44, C45, F1/F2/F6, N2/N3/N8/N12, W4, W12, P6 | OPEN — **drivers listed; to be priced at 5,000/yr** |

## R — Reliability, safety, verification

| ID | Criterion | Type | Value | Basis / source | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | Reliability target (per round) | Chosen | | | OPEN |
| R2 | Dud rate | Chosen | | From F10 | OPEN |
| R3 | Built-in-test coverage | Chosen | **DEFINED 2026-09-19 at H13 — coverage is tiered by where someone can act on it.** The **pre-launch go/no-go** must detect the failure modes that would prevent the mission (a stated, justified coverage target — it is the crew's only defence, and its action is the abort); the **in-flight BIT is not a coverage claim at all**: its requirement is to *classify* a fault well enough to select the correct response (hold / derate / fuze-autonomous, H13) and to log the evidence (R10). Verification: the fault-class table at P4, with R5's campaign and the R7 rig exercising the flight classes | H13 (closes it), F12 (go/no-go), R4, R10, R7 | DEFINED — closed at H13, 2026-09-19 |
| R4 | Graceful degradation defined per failure mode | Chosen | **DEFINED 2026-09-19 at H13 — the response set is fixed: pre-launch, the abort (R3's go/no-go); in flight, three graded responses and no fourth — hold (H2's last valid command), derate (H9's limit multiplier) and fuze-autonomous (F1) — with the rule "no mode may be worse than unguided" as the acceptance test applied to every fault class.** The reserve/arming path (W5, F6, H12) sits outside the guidance domain's fault set by construction, so the failure direction of a guidance fault is a lost correction, never a lost fuze or a hazard | H13 (fixes the set), F1, F6, H2, H9, H10, H12, R3, risk 26 | DEFINED — the set is fixed at H13; **the per-class table remains the verification artifact (P4/R5)** |
| R5 | Monte Carlo: number of runs, input distributions, confidence level | Chosen | **Claimed — a two-tier campaign, with the confidence convention as part of the requirement.** **Tier 1: 5,000 runs at the design condition** (6M, maximum range, standard atmosphere) — the headline CEP at **±2.0 % (95 % confidence)**. **Tier 2: 1,000 runs at each of five envelope corners** (3M, cold, hot, high wind, worst navigation case) — corners resolved to **±4.5 % (95 %)**. **≈10,000 runs total, each a full 7-DOF flight with the guidance law in the loop.** Statistical basis: for a Rayleigh radial distribution the sample median's relative standard error is ≈ **0.72/√N** (N = 1,000 → ±4.5 %; 5,000 → ±2.0 %; 10,000 → ±1.4 %), and the claim's margin is 6.1 m — so **the binding uncertainty is the coefficient set (risk 8), not the sample size**, which is why the spend goes to conditions rather than to one giant sample. **Input distributions:** nav **3.7 m** (3.0 + 2.0 + 1.0), IPP propagation and model **13 m**, ring angle **9 m**, actuation and loop lag **6 m**, met residual **5 m**, aero/ballistic **8 m**, fuze **3 m**, plus launch perturbations (tip-off, initial yaw, muzzle-velocity spread `[ref]`). **The two model-error terms (13 m and 8 m) shall be sampled with a stated correlation, not independently** — risk 16's double-count is settled here, and the correlation value must be justified from the coefficient set. **Target-location error is switched separately (0 / 10 / 25 m) for the M5 sensitivity and never enters the headline CEP.** **Confidence convention: the published CEP is the point estimate with its 95 % CI, and the claim (CEP ≤ 30 m) is supported by the worst envelope corner's 95 % upper bound, not by the design-point estimate.** **Added 2026-09-19 — the campaign publishes a breach fraction alongside the CEP.** The second estimate to quote is **the fraction of rounds whose miss exceeds 30 m, per condition**, because that is where this design's risk actually sits: M7's authority margin is 1.11:1 and G3's gate removes the apogee band, so the *median* may close comfortably while the *tail* does not. The predecessor study in this project is the worked example — median CEP50 **21.5 m** over 250 runs, but **p90 33 m, worst ≈150 m, and 28 % of runs breaching 30 m under a systematic drag error**, with its stress case **authority-limited (≈105 m) rather than algorithm-limited.** R5's stated outputs are therefore: **CEP, its 95 % CI, the worst corner's 95 % upper bound, and the breach fraction per condition** — and M5's published claim form carries the same two numbers, so the tail is visible rather than assumed away. *(The breach fraction is an output of the same campaign — no extra runs, no change to the tiers.)* | User choice 2026-09-18 (option (a) + the convention). Statistics: sample-median standard error for a Rayleigh population, 1/(2·f(r₅₀)·√N) with f(r₅₀) = 0.5887/σ → relative SE ≈ 0.72/√N. Closes G4 and addresses risk 16; carries the M5 sensitivity. **Harness named 2026-09-19 (decision log): RocketPy's Monte Carlo layer executes this campaign — the definition above is unchanged by that decision, and it inherits the obligation to justify the correlation from the coefficient set rather than from the tool's defaults** | DEFINED 2026-09-18 — **the campaign is specified; the runs are P3's** |
| R6 | CEP demonstration method (rounds, conditions, range) | Chosen | | For the trials plan | OPEN |
| R7 | Bench rig capability specification | Chosen | **Claimed — the capability specification is set now; the rig is built only if the entry is shortlisted (user decision 2026-09-18), and the user's instruction is that it shall showcase the full capability of the design rather than a scaled-down demonstration.** **Build gate: P7, post-selection** — the submission presents this as a **proof-of-concept plan, never as an existing asset**, and R7's hardware is the track's one explicitly parked item (reason: build cost and schedule against the 30 Sep deadline; P7 is the proof-of-concept phase by definition). **Capability list the rig must meet:** **(1) ring friction torque versus relative speed at flight-representative speed** — up to ≈2 000 rad/s, against the reference datum's 83.8 rad/s (a 19.4× scaling, risk 24); this is C13's flatness spec and C25's datum, and it verifies the C23 preload trade. **(2) The machine as actuator and generator at flight relative speed** — the **2.92 kW** generation path with the dump stage (C42/W7/W12) and the supercapacitor buffer (W4), against the reference's 400 W bank. **(3) Closed-loop ring-angle tracking** under an imposed aerodynamic torque profile, with the DOB, state-dependent limits and clamping — **overshoot ≤ 10 % and never past δ (G11), which is the rig that fixes G11's provisional δ.** **(4) Magnetometer coexistence** with the alternator at speed — F15's coherent notch and current compensation. **(5) The 24 GHz detector's spin-modulation rejection**, sensor spinning at 176–304 Hz (F2/F3, risk 6). **(6) The ring–body power/data interface** (risk 34) and a setback-representative pulse on the buffer subassembly (risk 35). **(7) The fuze-well thermal interface**, measured in W/K on a representative joint (thread + seat + bore) against W7/P6's ≥ 58 / ≥ 20–27 W/K. **Mechanical realisation: spin the ring (rotor) at flight relative speed with the stator fixed** — same relative speed gives the same friction, EMF, iron loss and generated power (`P = T·ω_rel`) — rim ≈ 143 m/s at Ø151 mm and 2 000 rad/s, so balance and containment are real; the aero torque is always emulated, because still air has no canard torque. **Structure (the assistant's proposal, not the user's instruction): two rigs** — a new high-speed relative-spin rig for (1)(2)(4)(5)(6)(7) and the reference's validated low-speed dynamometer for (3), whose methodology is thereby not rehosted onto the harder machine | User decision 2026-09-18 (build deferred to post-selection; full-capability specification). Inherited requirements from C13/C25/risk 24, C42/W7/W12, G11–G15, F2/F3, F15, risks 6, 34, 35, and P6's thermal check. Not R7's: the 15 000 g launch survival itself (E4) | **DEFINED 2026-09-18 — specification set; hardware parked at P7, gated on shortlisting.** Consequence carried: the items verified here (C25, C23's trade, risks 6/24/34/35) stay **declared open verifications** in the submission rather than demonstrated results |
| R8 | Hardware-in-the-loop definition | Chosen | | Ring angle must feed back into flight | OPEN |
| R9 | TRL target for the submission | Chosen | | PS prefers a prototype | OPEN |
| R10 | Post-flight logging and evidence | Chosen | | Data for trials and for the submission | OPEN |

## X — Growth module: terminal seeker (optional)

*Added 2026-09-17 from the team's proposal to add a laser/EO terminal seeker for GPS-denied operation. This block exists so the decision is tracked and decided rather than living in conversation. **X1 was decided 2026-09-18: the module stays out of the baseline.** The criteria below now bound what it could claim if it were offered as an option — nothing here changes any M, G, C, F, N, W, E, K or R criterion.*

| ID | Criterion | Type | Value | Basis / source | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| X1 | ⚑ Role of the terminal seeker: baseline capability or optional growth module | Chosen | **Claimed — optional growth module, out of the baseline.** The kit is designed with a defined mechanical and electrical interface for a future terminal seeker (X18); the baseline carries none of the module's mass, power or cost, and retains the one-moving-part / no-actuator / no-in-flight-battery story that the SWaP-C argument rests on. The submission may describe the module **as a bounded option and never as a delivered capability** — the bound being X4's correctable miss against M7's 407 m requirement (X5) | User decision 2026-09-18 (option A, the assistant's recommendation). PS requires "modular design suitable for future upgrades": the decision answers that clause with a **defined interface** rather than a performance claim. Risks 29–31 are the stated bounds; risk 30's mitigation is this decision | DEFINED |
| X2 | ⚑ Seeker guidance mode (semi-active laser spot homing / EO scene matching / beacon aiding) | Chosen | | Depends on X1 — now decidable as an option rather than a baseline fit | OPEN |
| X3 | ⚑ Geometric terminal window — the flight phase in which a target-referenced seeker's field of view contains the target | Derived | **Derived — the window is set by trajectory geometry, not by the seeker.** The body axis lies within a few degrees of the velocity vector (S4's ≤ 3° yaw, S5's trim), so the target is in view exactly when its off-axis angle — the line-of-sight depression to the target, less the body pitch — falls inside the aperture half-angle φ. Over the M4 design trajectory that angle is **70–90° and increasing throughout the ascent**, **≈33–44° at apogee**, and falls monotonically to ≈ 0 at impact. Two structural results, both robust to the trajectory model: **(a) no target-referenced guidance exists anywhere in the ascent — a field-of-view limit, independent of where the designator sits, which is the register's original finding now reproduced from geometry rather than asserted; (b) the window opens in the descent at a time set by φ** — 45° → apogee (≈ 45 s to impact); 30° → ≈ 35 s; **20° → ≈ 20 s (working case)**; 10° → ≈ 10 s | Derived from the M4 design trajectory (apogee t ≈ 41 s, impact t ≈ 86 s) using two bracketing models — a vacuum-equivalent descent from the `[ref]` apogee/impact times, and a constant-impact-angle descent — which agree on the structure and bracket the edges by ≈ ±30 %. Aperture half-angle is fixed at X10/X11. **The exact edge is a P3 output and moves with the aero set (risk 8)** | DERIVED — provisional (aero set; φ from X10/X11) |
| X4 | ⚑ Correctable miss inside the terminal window (ZEM_max) | Derived | **Derived — and the register's stored pair was internally inconsistent.** The correctable miss is a **divert**: `z_max = a·t_go²/N` with `a = F/m = 20/45 =` **0.444 m/s²** [M7, C8]. Pure-divert bound (N = 2, full force held across the whole window): **10 s → 22 m; 15 s → 50 m; 20 s → 89 m.** A ZEM-based terminal law with effective gain N = 3 corrects less: **15 / 33 / 59 m**. **Design point, on X3's 20 s window: ≈ 59 m (N = 3), bounded above by 89 m (N = 2).** **Correction recorded: the register previously carried "≈ 35 m at 15 s, ≈ 90 m at 20 s" — those two figures mix N = 3 and N = 2 and do not come from one law. The pair must not be quoted together**, and neither figure should reappear in the submission | Derived from M7 (a = F/m with F = 20 N **[ref]**, m = 45.0 kg reference-model design point) and X3 (t_go). **Supersedes the register's original placeholder pair.** Confirmed against the P3 terminal-phase simulation | DERIVED — provisional (X3 window; N fixed by the terminal law at X2) |
| X5 | Midcourse error the seeker cannot remove — the part that still requires GNSS/INS | Derived | **≈ 348 m of the 407 m requirement — 85 % — is beyond the seeker's reach.** X4's 59 m design-point divert is the largest miss a terminal seeker can null; the remaining **407 − 59 = 348 m** must still be delivered by GNSS/INS midcourse guidance. Under a GNSS outage the seeker therefore narrows the error from all of it to the midcourse residual — **it does not close it**, and it cannot substitute for midcourse navigation at any aperture | Derived from M7 (407 m) less X4 (59 m). Confirms risk 29 with arithmetic rather than assertion | DERIVED — provisional (M7 anchor; X3/X4) |
| X6 | Terminal window duration, FOV entry to impact | Derived | **≈ 20 s at the working aperture (φ = 20°)** — ≈ 10 s at φ = 10°, ≈ 35 s at φ = 30°, and up to ≈ 45 s for a wide-FOV imaging aperture that sees the target from apogee. The duration is the direct input to X4's divert, so **aperture half-angle is the single number that sets what the module can claim** | Derived from X3 | DERIVED — provisional (φ from X10/X11) |
| X7 | Seeker mounting and roll-decoupling approach (de-spun ring vs a separate de-rolled gimbal) | Chosen | | The ring is already roll-decoupled — the same argument that places the IMU on it | OPEN |
| X8 | Residual roll rate and image rotation the sensor must tolerate, with compensation | Derived | | Body 176–304 Hz [S6]; ring slew ±31.4 rad/s [G7] is measured and known | OPEN |
| X9 | ⚑ Fuze / nose real-estate conflict — seeker aperture versus proximity aperture, impact switch and S&A | Chosen | | Both want the tip. Physical conflict with F2 and F6 | OPEN |
| X10 | Aperture and window design — band, material, rain erosion, thermal, cleanliness | Chosen | | | OPEN |
| X11 | Detector type, band and laser PRF coding | Chosen | | SAL quadrant detector at 1.06 µm class versus an imaging array | OPEN |
| X12 | Processing location and compute budget | Chosen | | Feature/vision processing inside 15 000 g and the SWaP-C envelope | OPEN |
| X13 | Module mass and power | Derived | | Feeds P9 and W1 | OPEN |
| X14 | Designator dependency and the resulting change to the employment concept | Chosen | | Requires an observer with line of sight and a designator; a drop-in kit does not | OPEN |
| X15 | Weather and obscurant limitation, stated honestly against GNSS | Derived | | Laser does not penetrate cloud; GNSS does. The two fail in different conditions, so the seeker is not a like-for-like substitute | OPEN |
| X16 | ⚑ Cost delta versus the baseline kit | Derived | | Feeds K4; the PS premise is "low cost" | OPEN |
| X17 | ⚑ Primary GPS-denied answer: anti-jam GNSS with INS coasting as the baseline, seeker as the optional secondary | Chosen | **Claimed — anti-jam GNSS with INS coasting is the baseline GPS-denied answer; the seeker is the optional secondary and carries no GPS-denied capability claim.** The mechanism is half-built already: **N3's aperture is multi-element on the spin axis with its elements combined coherently**, so spatial nulling against a jammer is a signal-processing addition to an aperture the design already has, not a new antenna. INS coasting is bounded by N4's outage tolerance, which N1 already owes — with the derived statement that unaided error is **≈110 m over 50 s from 0.1° of attitude error**, so coasting **degrades** the CEP and never holds it | User decision 2026-09-18 (option A, the assistant's recommendation). The fielded analogue (**M1156 PGK E5**) answers GPS denial with **anti-jam electronics in the same envelope and the same cost class**, and needs no designator and no change to the employment concept — which keeps this inside the drop-in retrofit premise and the K4 cost lane. X5's arithmetic refutes the alternatives: the seeker nulls only **59 m of 407 m (15 %)**, so it cannot be the primary answer at any aperture | DEFINED — the requirement level (null depth, jamming-to-signal margin, bandwidth) is N5's to set, now as a **baseline requirement rather than a future upgrade** |
| X18 | Modular interface definition for the growth module — mechanical and electrical | Chosen | | Satisfies the PS modularity requirement and gives the submission a credible upgrade path | OPEN |
| X19 | Visual odometry: role and feasibility limits | Chosen / Derived | | At 10–15 km altitude over low-contrast terrain with cloud cover, monocular VO has no trackable features, and VO is relative rather than absolute. Plausible only as terminal impact-point refinement, where a ring-mounted camera is viable because roll is already removed. **Bounding data point (added 2026-09-19, from the predecessor study in this project — a worked EO terminal-navigation concept in this class):** a 2 MP global-shutter sensor, **40 µs gated exposure** so that a 233 rev/s body spin smears ≈0.9 % of a revolution per frame, eight wedge frames mosaicked per revolution on the nav core, scene-matched against reference imagery loaded at the setter, **≈11 J for an 8 s terminal window (≈1.4 W)**, position σ target ≤15 m, behind a sapphire window. Two lessons carry: the **roll channel must be good enough to timestamp the frames** (that study's critical path — here the ring mount already supplies it), and roll-gating works **without** de-spinning the camera, so a ring-mounted camera is a *convenience* for this kit rather than a necessity | OPEN |

**Note on X19 and X2.** The de-spun ring is the enabling platform for any imaging sensor here: a body-mounted camera would have its image rotating at the body spin rate (176–304 Hz), which makes optical flow unusable. This is the same architectural argument that places the IMU on the ring, and it should be stated as such if the growth module is described in the submission.

## Decision log

Record every Chosen criterion here with its rationale. This is the traceability that makes the submission read as engineering rather than assertion.

| Date | Criteria ID | Decision | Rationale | Trade-offs accepted |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2026-09-17 | M1 | Mixed general-purpose target set: point / light materiel primary, proximity-capable soft/area secondary (option D) | PS mandates a three-mode fuze, which only earns its place if soft and defilade targets are in scope; mirrors the M1156 role set; keeps CEP ≤ 30 m as the binding requirement | All three fuze modes stay live, so the setter data package (F12) and aimpoint definition (M5) carry more load; treatment of target-location error deferred to M5 |
| 2026-09-17 | M2 | All three fuze modes — proximity, time, impact — configurable before flight; full terminal-effect envelope retained, impact selectable superquick / short delay / graze (option B) | User instruction: the kit must offer all three fuze modes configurable pre-flight. Option B is the only choice that satisfies it without reducing the round's terminal effect, which the mixed general-purpose target set (M1) requires | Delay bounds, burst-height range and graze-angle limits deferred to F3/F5; compatibility of the explosive-train output with the shell booster becomes a hard F9 item |
| 2026-09-17 | M3 | Option C: full functional envelope across charges 3M–6M, with the CEP claim bounded to a range band set at M4; outside it accuracy degrades but never below unguided | The retrofit premise requires the charge system to be untouched, so full-envelope functionality is not optional; a CEP claim that cannot be verified across the whole band is a liability in trials; the binding loading case is low-q near apogee, so excluding charges buys almost nothing. Accepted by the user | Charge/velocity and range-band values remain **[ref]** placeholders; M3 stays OPEN-BLOCKED until YIL firing tables arrive |
| 2026-09-17 | M4 | Claim CEP ≤ 30 m at the maximum-range, full-charge (6M) condition; mid-band results reported as supporting data only | The kit earns its keep where unguided dispersion is largest, which is max range; the guided window is also longest there; bounding the claim at M3 keeps anything outside the band from being overstated. Accepted by the user | The numeric range is pending YIL firing tables (M4 stays OPEN-BLOCKED); a single formal trial condition, with no second formal claim |
| 2026-09-17 | M5 | CEP defined as the 2-D circular 50% radius; formal claim measured against the loaded aimpoint with target-location error excluded; end-to-end CEP against the true target published as a sensitivity with a named target-location error | Keeps the headline claim inside our control while never hiding the dependency — the bait-and-switch a defence reviewer probes for. Accepted by the user | One extra term in the Monte Carlo (R5) and an explicitly stated target-location assumption; the trials plan (R6) must instrument against a surveyed aimpoint |
| 2026-09-17 | M6 | Re-baselined from a bare "500 m class" assumption to a cited external anchor: unguided M549A1 155 mm CEP = 267 m at maximum range → equivalent circular 1σ = 227 m. Criterion deliberately kept OPEN-BLOCKED pending YIL's firing tables | M6 is the root of the sizing chain, so a guessed value propagates its error into every downstream requirement — not defensible for a number the submission will be judged against. An externally published, citable figure gives a real bound to size against now, and the CEP form settles the 1σ-versus-peak-to-peak ambiguity the YIL request was written around | The anchor is a foreign round at a foreign range (30.1 km from the M198); if YIL's dispersion differs materially the whole chain moves. The range/deflection split is still unknown and YIL request 3 stays live. Logged as risk 12 |
| 2026-09-17 | M7 | Re-derived as a capture-fraction problem rather than a percentile lookup: required authority A = σ·√(−2·ln(1−P)) with σ = 227 m, design capture fraction **P = 80%** → **A = 407 m**, equivalent Δv = 19.5 m/s over a 41.8 s full-force burn. Requirement split into range-direction and deflection components and stated as not interchangeable | A percentile lookup answers "how big is the 50% radius" — the wrong question. What the kit must buy is the fraction of the unguided population the loop can capture. Below 80% the uncorrected tail, not the guided cluster, sets the achievable CEP, so the 30 m claim stops describing our guidance. 80% is the largest capture fraction that stays inside the authority available: P = 85% (442 m) leaves only a 1.07:1 margin and P = 90% (487 m) exceeds what a 20 N force can deliver across the terminal window | Margin is 1.16:1 and assumes continuous full-force authority across the whole 45 s window that G3 says is not uniformly available. Range correction costs more authority than deflection, so the isotropic Rayleigh assumption may be optimistic. Logged as risks 10 and 11 |
| 2026-09-17 | M8 | Effectiveness metric derived as rounds for a 90 % probability of defeat against the primary target class at effective radius r_eff = 30 m — 4 guided rounds vs ≈264 unguided (≈66×), with r_eff 20 m and 50 m published as sensitivity | The metric turns the CEP claim into the operator-level payoff the impact story is scored on; 30 m matches M1's primary target set (point / light materiel) and sits mid-band in the sensitivity, so the headline is neither the softest nor the harshest case. Accepted by the user | Unguided baseline assumes independent rounds and no aim correction — practical observed fire narrows the ratio, so it is an upper bound. r_eff is unverified pending lethality data, and the whole metric inherits the M6 anchor (risks 12, 14) |
| 2026-09-17 | G1 | Continuous impact-point prediction (IPP), with the apogee no-command blackout and a systematic-bias trim term folded in as provisions of the law rather than as separate options (option A) | User choice. IPP is the only candidate that fits M7's 1.16:1 authority margin — it spends authority in proportion to the miss it can actually remove, rather than holding an uploaded path (B) or deferring every correction to a terminal phase with less time and no less demand (C). It closes on measured state instead of assumed state, so it absorbs muzzle-velocity spread, met error and launch perturbation rather than trusting the pre-flight model, and it drives the inherited ≈6 Hz ring-angle command channel with no new actuation. The reference work has no guidance law at all, so this is also the novelty claim | Continuous IPP makes two inputs the kit does not yet own into hard dependencies — target coordinates and met data through the gun-line setter (F11/F12, both blocked) and a credible Mach × α × β aero set (risk 8). Its residual error is the G4 allocation, and it must hold the 15 m the CEP budget assigns to guidance and control. Logged as risk 13 |
| 2026-09-17 | G3 | State-dependent authority gate (option B): the law runs continuously and an onboard estimate of the available guidance force gates the command, so the inhibited band is computed in flight rather than pre-declared. Guided phase runs from max(arming distance, GNSS convergence) — placeholder t ≈ 15 s — to impact at t ≈ 86 s, with the band provisionally at t ≈ 35–55 s | A fixed band (A) is only correct at the elevation and charge it was drawn for, and the reference data shows the collapse moving between t = 40 s and 55 s across four elevations — so a static band is wrong at most conditions and can command into a dead zone. Option C is refuted outright: the same fixed-limit approach lost control between t = 30 s and 50 s on hardware. Option B is correct across the envelope and makes G3, G14 and G15 one state-dependent mechanism, which is the lesson the reference work paid for | The gate is only as good as the onboard force estimate, so it inherits risk 8 and gains a failure mode of its own — an optimistic estimate reintroduces the exact loss of control the gate exists to prevent. More verification depth, not more design risk. The t ≈ 15 s opening edge is the assistant's derived placeholder, **not** a user instruction, and stays provisional pending F8 and N2/N3. Logged as risk 15 |
| 2026-09-17 | G4 | Adopted option C — the brief's error budget re-cut for IPP at the same ≈24 m headline: nav 5, guidance & control 17 (propagation 13, ring angle 9, actuation 6), meteo 5, ballistic/aero 8, fuze 3, with the muzzle-velocity residual absorbed into the navigation state. RSS 20.3 m → CEP 23.9 m, ≈20 % margin under the claim | G1 changed two lines of the original skeleton: continuous IPP measures the actual velocity, so a muzzle-velocity error already incurred sits inside the nav state rather than being a modelling residual, and met error shrinks from whole-trajectory to the forecast over the remaining flight. The released budget belongs to the guidance term, which now owns the dominant error. Option B would have kept the headline while under-budgeting the dominating term and hiding where the risk moved; option A would have spent the entire margin for nothing. Accepted by the user | The allocation is a top-down split and nothing in it is analysed yet — it closes only when R5 runs with the loop in the loop. The IPP propagation term and the ballistic/aero residual overlap (both are model and coefficient error) and are probably correlated, so the RSS may understate the total; they must be separated when R5's distributions are defined. Logged as risk 16 |
| 2026-09-17 | G6 | Absolute ring-angle accuracy set at **≤ 1.0° (1σ)**, earth-referenced, with the earth reference taken from a magnetometer fused into the nav solution rather than from a separate absolute sensor (option A). Paired with G5 ≤ 0.8°, giving an RSS of 1.28° → 9.1 m and closing the G4 ring-angle line | Option A closes the G4 allocation exactly (1.28° → 9.1 m) rather than with slack nobody identified a need for, and needs no hardware beyond the nav filter already required for N1/N14. Its failure mode is graceful: if N12 cannot hold 1°, the design degrades to option B's numbers — ring angle 15.3 m, guidance term 21.0 m, CEP 27.9 m, ≈7 % margin — rather than needing a redesign. Option C would have bought margin by adding a second absolute sensor inside the alternator's field, i.e. more failure modes for accuracy we had not shown a need for. Accepted by the user | The spec is filter-dependent, so it holds only after nav convergence and inherits G3's t ≈ 15 s placeholder. The crux is disturbance rejection rather than sensor resolution, which turns in-flight calibration against the alternator's phase-dependent stray field into a hard N12 requirement. Logged under risk 2 |
| 2026-09-17 | G15 | Layered state-dependent limit law (option A): `I_lim = min(2·Î_disturbance, I_avail(ω_rel))` and `V_lim = 26 rad/s per A × I_lim`, keeping both independent limit routes rather than either alone | The reference evidence shows two distinct causes and they need two distinct guards: the DOB route covers a Taero excursion the ring must arrest (the observed overshoot to ≈5.5 rad), the capability route covers the supply collapse as relative speed falls — the Fig. 7.4 region where demand exceeded available current and control was lost outright. Either alone leaves one of the two observed failure routes open. Critically, the capability half is measured from relative speed rather than modelled, so it is the one limit in the design that does not inherit the aero-data dependency of risks 8 and 15. Accepted by the user | More to verify than either single-route option: needs the DOB (G14), an alternator capability map (C18) and a relative-speed measurement (N9/N10). Also makes G3, G14 and G15 one coupled mechanism, so a defect in the DOB is now a defect in all three |
| 2026-09-17 | G14 | Disturbance observer filter bandwidth kept at the reference value `Q(s) = 1/(0.5s + 1)` — ≈0.32 Hz corner, ≈1.1 s rise (option A) | Nothing in the disturbance spectrum justifies a faster filter: Taero and Tf have no high-frequency content, and the fastest real excursion (the apogee collapse out to the 2.12 N·m descent torque) develops over tens of seconds. The slow filter is also what keeps the G15 hard limit stable — mixing estimate noise into a hard clamp on a 1 kHz loop, in a system with clamping anti-windup and a known non-linear runaway mode, would be reintroducing the disease the DOB was prescribed for. Option B buys responsiveness with no evidence of need and forfeits the hardware validation; option C trades away authority against an M7 margin that is already short. Accepted by the user | The DOB output is accepted as a filtered estimate, not a measurement, so the 2× factor in the current limit must be justified as a bound — an open item and the knob to turn if the rig shows the limit lagging the collapse. Reopening the filter bandwidth would reopen the noise path instead. Coupling logged as risk 17 |
| 2026-09-17 | G2 | Guidance law output is a commanded earth-referenced ring angle θ_gk, fed as a position reference to the outer position loop, with the guidance rate proposed at 10 Hz (provisional). Force magnitude is not commanded, because it cannot be | Checking the reference architecture settled that the ring angle is the *only* command channel: steering comes from cant angle rather than deflection, so the force magnitude is set by cant angle and dynamic pressure and the ring merely aims it. Option B would put a force → angle inversion inside the loop and have the controller reconstruct G3's gate and C18's capability map to produce a magnitude the guidance law cannot actually set; option C would command a magnitude that fixed-incidence canards cannot accept. Option A is the only one the hardware can act on, and it preserves the validated cascade interface unchanged. Accepted by the user | The always-on steering force becomes an explicit obligation on G1's propagation model and hence on G4's 13 m term — logged as risk 18. The 10 Hz guidance rate is the assistant's provisional placeholder, **not** a user instruction, and needs the loop model to confirm |
| 2026-09-17 | G11 | Overshoot requirement set as a hard no-runaway constraint plus a margin-measuring overshoot target (option A), with a guard band δ = 0.1π rad ≈ 18° proposed by the assistant. Rig metric: overshoot ≤ 10 % of step and never exceeding δ | The requirement is written as the failure mechanism rather than a proxy for it. The reference's escalation path is explicit — large overshoot → full rotation → non-linearity → instability — and its fixed-limit controller spiked to ≈5.5 rad against a 2.4–3.9 rad reference, so a percentage-of-step spec alone would have permitted the failure: 10 % on a 3.9 rad step is harmless, the same 10 % on a step near the boundary wraps. Option C would leave nothing to verify against on the rig. Accepted by the user | δ is the assistant's proposed value and stays provisional until R7 or analysis on the actual loop; how the reference representation is handled for setpoints near the boundary is a design detail carried into R7. The requirement is only met if G12, G14 and G15 hold, so it adds another consumer to the risk-17 coupling |
| 2026-09-17 | G10 | Outer position loop settling set at 0.103 s to the 2 % band, derived from the retained 6.2 Hz bandwidth. **Corrects G2: the guidance rate moves from a 10 Hz floor to a ≤ 10 Hz ceiling with ~6 Hz as the design point** | The retained cascade settles in 4τ at 6.2 Hz, and a 10 Hz guidance update re-commands exactly as fast as the loop can settle — so it never rests between commands. Making the guidance rate a consequence of the loop's own speed rather than an independent pick is what turns it from a guess into a requirement, and ~6 Hz is the reference's own picture of the ring-angle command channel. Option B would have needed a ~100 Hz inner loop against 33.6 Hz validated and would have fought the deliberately slow DOB from G14; option C would have left R7 and the electronics design with nothing to close against. Accepted by the user | The 0.103 s figure takes a first-order-dominant response assumption, reasonable for a P outer over a PI inner loop 5.4× faster. The derived process load — ~300 IPP propagations per flight — has no criterion owning the flight computer, so it is tracked only as risk 19. Recorded alongside this decision: G7, G8 and G9 are now closed as consequences rather than open questions |
| 2026-09-17 | G12 | Clamping (conditional integration) retained as the anti-windup method (option A), with the implementation constraint that clamping is evaluated against the live state-dependent limit every 1 ms cycle | Clamping is half of the reference's *combined* result — DOB, dynamic limits and clamping were validated together and produced no overshoot and a symmetric response on hardware. Unpicking part of a validated combination to buy a smoothing property nothing has called for is the same trade already declined at G14. But G15's limits move continuously, so a latched limit would leave the integrator frozen against a stale threshold — the live evaluation is what makes the retained method correct in the system we have actually built, not the system the reference built. Option B costs a tuning gain and more fixed-point arithmetic at 1 kHz on a processor already carrying ~300 propagations; option C reintroduces a discontinuity into a loop G11 now requires never to overshoot past its guard band. Accepted by the user | The frozen-integrator-above-a-falling-limit behaviour is accepted as a residual and carried into R7 for verification rather than argued closed. Extends the risk-17 coupling to a fourth consumer of the same estimate |
| 2026-09-17 | G13 | Control loop rate kept at 1 kHz, with the control loop in FPGA fabric and the guidance law plus nav filter on the processor (option A) | The rate itself is not really open — 1 kHz is 30× the inner bandwidth and the DOB was discretised at 1 ms, so it is part of the validated artefact. The decision worth taking is the task split, because G10 loaded ~300 IPP propagations onto the flight processor with nothing owning it (risk 19). Splitting at the hardware boundary makes control timing independent of that load and converts a hard 1 ms deadline into a comfortable ~0.17 s one. Option B would let the propagation load jitter a 1 kHz loop and so degrade G11's overshoot margin and G12's live-limit evaluation — trading a compute budget for a stability margin in the one domain where we have already been shown what instability looks like. Option C forfeits the validated no-overshoot result. Accepted by the user | Risk 19 is reduced but not closed: the requirement is now stated and measurable, yet it still has no criterion of its own owning the processor's throughput, memory and timing margin. | 2026-09-17 | C9 | **Symmetric torque criterion corrected and made state-dependent: `Taero ≤ Talt_max(ω_rel)/2 + Tf`, evaluated at the instantaneous available current.** The inherited `(Talt_max + Tf)/2` is corrected to the exact torque balance (2.91 → 2.96 N·m at 8 A), and the single number is replaced by a curve — **3.68 N·m at launch (10 A), 2.96 at the 8 A design point, 1.89 at apogee (5 A)** | Setting the two torque directions equal — `+X = Talt_max + Tf − Taero` against `−X = Taero − Tf` — is what the criterion actually means, and the published form is symmetric only to within 3.5 % because it drops one friction term. More importantly, `Talt_max = Kt·I_avail(ω_rel)` is not a constant: the available current falls from ≈10 A to ≈5 A as relative speed decays, so a single number is only ever true at one point in flight. Applying the curve is not extra conservatism — it is the same criterion evaluated honestly, and it is the register's own **G15 principle (every limit state-dependent by construction)** applied to the sizing equation rather than only to the controller. It also gives C18/C19 a reason to exist beyond "cover the load". Accepted by the user | The correction moves the criterion *down* at apogee to 1.89 N·m and exposes that **the binding case is descent, not apogee**: at `Taero = 2.119 N·m` (t = 86.5 s) the criterion demands 5.64 A against ≈5 A available — an **11 % shortfall in transient authority at the end of flight**. Holding authority is unaffected (2.82 A needed), so this is reduced transient margin rather than loss of control, but it is now a requirement with two owners — the alternator's low-relative-speed current (C18/C19) or the control-pair cant (C3). Logged as risk 23. The curve rests on the `[ref]` current profile, so it is provisional on C18 |

| 2026-09-17 | F1 | **All three PS modes with a full setting envelope — proximity burst height 2–15 m (≈7 m default), time 0.1–199.9 s at 0.1 s resolution, impact superquick / short delay ≈0.05 s / graze — plus an in-flight re-synchronisation of the time setting from the nav solution (option A)** | The PS names three modes and M2 requires the terminal-effect envelope of the fuze replaced to be retained, so the mode set was not the open question — the *ranges* were, and they are now numbers the rest of the F domain can be designed against. The in-flight time correction is the part that makes the fuze genuinely smart and it is specific to this architecture: a guided round's time of flight is changed by the guidance itself, so a pre-set time inherits the whole time-of-flight prediction error, whereas re-synchronising against a navigation solution the kit already carries makes the airburst track the actual trajectory. It costs almost nothing — the kit already has GNSS time, a nav filter and a data path to the nose | The correction is a **new failure path**: an erroneous time-of-flight correction bursts the round at the wrong height, silently, because the guidance law believes it hit the target. Bounded by making the interface one-way, the corrections bounded and gated on arming, and the fallback the pre-set time — so the degradation floor is the fuze being replaced, which is R4's "no mode worse than unguided" test applied to the fuze (risk 26).
