| 2026-09-17 | F2 | **RF Doppler proximity sensing at 24 GHz class (ISM), radiating through a nose radome, no optical window; the spin modulation of the ground return stated as a detection requirement (option A)** | The multi-mode fuze needs a sensor that works between 2 and 15 m over any ground, in any weather, from a nose that also has to survive the bore and Mach 2 heating. RF Doppler is the artillery norm for exactly that reason: it is independent of weather and obscurants in a way optics are not, it needs no window in the hottest and most abraded surface on the kit, and at 24 GHz the front end is COTS — which is the cost lane K4 depends on. The frequency was chosen as much for what it is *far from* as for what it is: the alternator's electrical content is only ≈2.1 kHz and GNSS sits at 1.2/1.5 GHz, so the sensor neither desensitises the receiver nor is desensitised by the machine sharing its volume. That is what reduces F15 from a general coexistence problem to one specific source — the converter's switching harmonics | The sensor **spins with the body at 176.3–304.4 Hz**, so the ground return is modulated by rotation and a threshold that ignores it bursts at the wrong height — a silent failure of the same family as risk 26, now stated as a requirement and handed to F3 (mechanism) and R7 (verification with spin applied). Rejected Ka-band (B) — pays attenuation and unit cost for accuracy we have not shown we need; rejected IR (C) — trades an RF problem for a window in the worst available location; rejected a nav-triggered airburst (D) — makes a safety-critical function depend on the navigation solution, against F1's autonomy provision, and neither GNSS altitude nor baro drift holds 2–15 m. | 2026-09-17 | F6 | **MIL-STD-1316 out-of-line rotor released by two independent mechanical stimuli — setback (15 000 g) and spin (176.3–304.4 Hz) — with the electrical arm command as a third, independent condition; the rotor is driven into line mechanically, not electrically (option A)** | MIL-STD-1316 asks for an out-of-line interruption of the explosive train with two independent safing mechanisms, and the launch environment supplies exactly two that the register has **already quantified** — so the safing case is built from numbers we own rather than invented thresholds, and F7 inherits real anchors for free. The mechanical drive is what puts the failure direction right: dead electronics, a dead battery or an absent launch stimulus all give a **dud rather than a premature burst**, and a misfire is safe with no abort logic to get wrong. Rejected B — one mechanical stimulus puts half the safety case on the electronics, against the two-independent-mechanism rule. Rejected C — electrically actuated arming depends on the reserve battery and the electronics being healthy at a single instant, needs abort logic, and adds failure modes where a reviewer looks hardest. **New consequence recorded:** the nose now carries five subsystems decided in four domains and **no criterion owns the packing** — logged as risk 27 |

Delivery had to be through the **existing** inductive interface, because F11's whole compatibility argument is that the kit requires no new gun-line equipment — and the fielded predecessor's setter already carries target and GNSS data, so the class interface is demonstrably capable. The setter's data capacity is now a named item in F11's post-selection confirmation, with split programming as the stated fallback rather than a redesign (option B rejected for the two-step mismatch mode and for weakening the no-new-equipment premise; option C rejected outright — it breaks G1 and wastes the G4 allocation). | 2026-09-17 | F15 | **Coexistence by frequency and time partitioning, not by shielding: the rotating magnetic term (1 234–2 131 Hz = 7 pole pairs × the measured relative rotation) rejected by a coherent notch, the current-proportional term compensated against measured coil current, and the converter's switching spectrum specified (≥ 100 kHz, controlled edge rate, L-band attenuation)** — with the derived negative result that the magnetometer's case cannot be solved spatially | The useful move was to stop treating this as one EMI problem and split it by *coupling path*, because each path turned out to be a function of something the kit already measures. The rotating field's frequency **is** the relative rotation that N9/N10 measures for the alternator, so its rejection is a coherent notch rather than a blind filter that would have to be slow enough to eat the signal. The drifting field is proportional to **coil current**, which C9/C18 also measures, so it is compensated in the in-flight calibration model — which is precisely what G6's "calibration must be in-flight, not pre-flight" was asking for and could not previously say how to do. Both mechanisms therefore **consume no new hardware and no new sensor**: they use signals the design already needs for the limit laws and the torque criterion | The honest finding, and the one worth putting in front of a reviewer, is the **negative result**: interference and measurand share the same band *and* the same space for the magnetometer, so no amount of shielding separates them — shielding-first would have looked rigorous, cost mass and volume in a nose that has none to spare (risk 27), and failed. The converter remains the only broadband source and is handled by specification and by E8, not by analysis. Verification is a rig run with the alternator at flight-representative speed, which R7 was already going to run for friction (risk 24). If the magnetometer itself proves unworkable, the escape route is priced from G6's own decision log — pre-launch alignment plus gyrocompassing, **CEP 27.9 m and a ≈7 % margin instead of 20 %** — so the fallback spends margin rather than requiring a redesign |

Deriving it bottom-up does two things beyond producing 3.7 m: it shows **which assumption each metre depends on** — the GNSS term on N3's antenna, the IMU term on N8's bias and ARW and on N6's mount — so the remaining work is traceable rather than implied; and it exposes the structure of the budget, which is that **the IMU is not carrying the navigation at all**. With aiding it supplies attitude and fills gaps; without aiding its error is two orders of magnitude larger than the allocation, because an attitude error mis-resolves the drag deceleration into a phantom acceleration that integrates twice | The honest consequence is the conditionality: **the 5 m line is a GNSS-availability claim, not an inertial-grade claim**, so it survives only while the receiver is tracking and **N4 now owes a bounded outage tolerance** rather than a graceful-degradation statement. The second consequence protects the architecture's own headline: the 2 m IMU term is only available on the de-spun ring, so **N1 is the quantitative backing for the slide-3 claim** that the ring converts the hardest sensing problem in this class into a conventional one. Recorded sensitivity: nav 5 → 10 m moves the CEP to 26.0 m and the margin to 13 %, so the line has real leverage and is worth the verification spend |
| 2026-09-17 | N3 | **A rotationally symmetric aperture on the spin axis, its elements combined rather than switched — so the spin produces no modulation by symmetry — with spin-phase-aided demodulation (option C) carried as the stated fallback** | The problem looks like a tracking-loop problem and is actually a geometry problem. The projectile spins about its own axis, and an antenna on that axis with a body-of-revolution pattern is **invariant under the rotation** — so there is nothing at 176.3–304.4 Hz to track out, no receiver redesign, no bandwidth traded against noise. Solving it by geometry is the cheapest answer available and the only one that leaves a standard receiver in place, which serves K4 directly. N15 closes with it: mission-side timestamp uncertainty ≤ 100 µs (1σ).** | N1's 1.0 m time/lever-arm term is the requirement this row answers, and at ≈ 900 m/s it needs ≈ 1 ms — so the kit must *know* when each sample was taken, not estimate it later. The split follows the consumers — the loop cannot wait on a mailbox for its feedback (G5, G11), while the navigation filter is content with ≥ 100 Hz IMU data that carries its own timestamps | **The ring link gains a timing obligation** (risk 34 extended): the IMU must stamp on the ring or the link's delay must be measured and bounded — flagged to H7, which owns the link. H2's mailbox contract now names ω_rel and the timestamp format. Cost accepted: a TCXO in the time base. No G, C, N, W or F decision changes |
| 2026-09-19 | H7 (risk 34's owner; discharges H6's timing obligation) | **The ring–body link is a rotary transformer for power and an optical (LED/photodiode) channel for data.** The ring only receives power — all generation is body-side — at ≈ 0.5–1.5 W; the IMU's data goes up optically, with a narrow down-channel reserved for configuration and the timestamp marker. N10's pickoff stays body-side, so the link carries data only and cannot disturb the loop. Risk 34's design half is retired; what remains is fit, mass and verification on R7 | Optics were chosen for two reasons that land in the same place: the channel is immune to the alternator's fields — F15's coexistence is fought in this exact volume and a magnetic data channel would sit beside N12's magnetometer — and the propagation delay is effectively zero, so H6's "stamp at conversion" holds with a measured, bounded correction instead of a ring-side clock. A capacitive channel varies with the gap (C35's 0.20 mm deflection becomes a modulation of the channel), and an inductive channel adds the magnetic source the magnetometer can least afford | **Remaining exposure carried into P4/R7, not glossed:** the ring-side assembly must fit C22's envelope, its mass lands on C26's 0.5 kg ring assumption and risk 33, and the optical alignment must hold across the 0.20 mm relative-deflection budget. No G, C, N, W or F decision changes |
| 2026-09-19 | H8 (with W8 and C17 resolved in the same pass) | **Keep the confirmed winding and set the bus below the flight-minimum EMF (option A): the machine-side four-quadrant stage is a buck in generation and a boost in motoring across the whole flight, with no buck↔boost crossing at any instant.** The bus becomes ≈ 150–200 V (value closing with the rails at H12); the machine sizing — Kt 0.362, Kv 0.209, C14–C19 — stands untouched; the stage closes the machine's *current* loop, so G15's limit law commands a converter setpoint rather than a load-bank resistance | The decision turned on an arithmetic fact rather than a preference: `E` falls from 400 V to ≈ 230 V as the spin decays, so **any bus inside that band is crossed once per flight** — and at the 400 V bus as previously chosen, the crossing lands on the launch condition, the point of peak load (2.92 kW). A converter asked to change character at the worst instant is the degeneracy G7 flagged; moving the bus below `E_min` removes it by construction. Route B (re-cut the winding for headroom at 400 V) is equally clean in principle but re-opens the confirmed machine and requires ≈ 800 V-class insulation and creepage inside a 19 mm slot at 15 000 g — buying with insulation what A buys with current, in the one envelope where insulation is hardest to hold | **The price is stated, not hidden:** bus-side current doubles at the peak (≈ 16.7 A at 175 V), which lands on the dump element (W12), the bus wiring and the low-voltage leg — and the dump's own protection policy is now the next question (H9). C17's turns count is not re-cut; its R and L remain winding-design outputs. No G, C, N or F decision changes |
| 2026-09-19 | H9 (with risk 17 extended) | **The dump is a chopper-driven shunt regulator on the H8 bus, and its first protection action is a deliberate derate of G15's current limit — authority traded for thermal margin, with the guidance law informed — and only continued thermal-margin loss trips it.** Thermometry on element and interface; chopper ≥ 100 kHz with controlled edges (F15); rating ≈ 16.7 A at 2.92 kW on the lowered bus | The tempting protection is the wrong one: cutting the dump when it gets hot collapses the machine's current, so the ring loses authority *without warning* and G15's law reacts to a collapse it did not command — a failure worse than the condition it protects against. Derating through the same limit law makes the response graceful, coordinated and consistent with how every other authority limit in this design already works (state-dependent by construction), and it keeps the price of overheating traceable to M7's margin (risk 10) instead of hidden | **Risk 17 extended:** the limit law now has a third driver (thermal margin) alongside the DOB estimate and the capability map, so a faulty margin estimate erodes authority in the same direction as a faulty DOB estimate — the coordination is what makes that safe, not isolation. Element rating, thermometry siting and trip threshold are P4 outputs; no G, C, N, W or F decision changes |
| 2026-09-19 | H10 | **The fuze keeps its own processor, board and power path; the guidance computer keeps its own — two processors with one physical, bounded boundary (option A).** The fuze MCU runs arming logic, mode logic, detector processing, the timer and the setter read-back in the reserve-battery domain; the only crossing is the bounded one-way time correction, with status returning. The fuze board becomes a named claimant in the spigot annulus | F1's autonomy provision was written as a *provision*; this decision makes it a *property*. With the function and its fallback (the pre-set time) living on the fuze's own silicon and power, a guidance-side fault cannot take the fuze's function away — and the safety case (arming, S&A) stays separable from flight-critical software, which is the separation a MIL-STD-1316 reviewer asks for first. The one-die route is refuted by H1's own argument: the M4F's timing guarantee (risk 36) exists because nothing else shares the loop's core | **Costs carried, not hidden:** a small MCU, board area in the annulus (risk 27's packing budget now names it), a few grams (risk 33) and its own regulation. **Constraint handed to H11:** the setter must program the fuze domain with the kit computer unpowered — the reserve domain is already alive pre-launch, so this is a placement rule, not a new power path. No G, C, N, W or F decision changes |
| 2026-09-19 | P3 toolchain — simulation harness and independent cross-check (serves S3, S5, S7, C11, N11; executes R5) | **Adopt RocketPy as the P3 harness and as a second, independent implementation — not as the governing trajectory model.** Reuse, in order of value: **(1) the Monte Carlo layer** — stochastic parameters, custom samplers, *correlated* sampling, rejection sampling and regression-based sensitivity — which runs R5's two-tier campaign; **(2) the environment model** — ISA 1976, custom profiles and soundings, and forecast / reanalysis / ensemble winds — supplying the atmosphere, the wind and the cold/hot/envelope corners R5's tiers are built from, and the density reference behind N11; **(3) the 6-DOF rigid-body integrator, coordinate frames, ground-impact event handling, plotting and the MATLAB bridge** — the skeleton for our own **7-DOF** model, and the second implementation every result is checked against. **The governing model stays ours:** 6-DOF rigid body plus the ring's independent roll DOF in the body-fixed-plane frame, carrying our **Mach × α × β** coefficient set, which enters through the generic-surface / individual-fin coefficient-injection path — whose cant-angle model already carries roll forcing and roll damping, the same physics as C1's steering and control pairs. MIT-licensed, so the fork is permitted; **version pinned at P3**. **Explicit non-use: no number in this register, and nothing in the submission, may be sourced from RocketPy's own results (risk 38).** | P3 was blocked on the aero set and carried **no named harness at all** — and the harness is separable from the coefficients, so it can be decided now and built while risk 8 is still open. RocketPy already provides the three things P3 would otherwise build from scratch: a Monte Carlo layer shaped for exactly the campaign R5 defines, a validated atmosphere and wind environment, and a 6-DOF integrator with event handling and coefficient injection. Two implementations are a **verification asset rather than a duplication** — agreement is evidence, disagreement locates a bug in one of them, which is the cheapest V&V this project will get — and its MATLAB bridge keeps cross-checks against the reference work's own toolchain possible. It costs no licence position and no schedule, because it is a harness and not a model | **Its validity envelope excludes this flight regime, and that is now risk 38:** small-spin roll approximation, Barrowman-linear aerodynamics, one rigid body with no ring DOF, no in-bore phase, and a published validation covering two flights peaking at 86 m/s and 173 m/s. So it cannot represent the de-spun ring, cannot compute our aerodynamics, and must never be quoted as the trajectory authority. **Consequence carried: this decision moves no criterion.** The S-row derivations and C11 still wait on the coefficient set (risk 8); R5's definition is unchanged and no tier, run count or corner moves; the campaign's compute cost is as R5 stated. The harness also inherits R5's obligation to justify the model-error correlation from the coefficient set rather than from its own defaults |
| 2026-09-19 | H11 (closing H1's crypto deferral; discharging H10's placement constraint) | **The package's keys authenticate the GNSS *signal* — authenticated navigation with the H755's crypto/hash engine — and the setter's inductive front end lives in the always-alive reserve domain, listened to independently by both domains, each validating its own read-back.** The H755 variant is selected (H1's deferral closed); the anti-spoof claim is conditional on the constellation's service and the receiver's support (F11/N2 confirmation, risk 39) | A spoofed fix is the one GNSS failure *worse* than jamming: it steers a guided round confidently to the wrong place, and no term in the G4 budget sees it — the same shape as risk 28's mis-entered coordinate, which the design already takes seriously enough to sanity-check. The keys were already in F12's package, so using them costs the part premium and a key path, not a new subsystem. The bus value falls out of the H8 rule (`below E_min`) with a control margin at the worst point, and it costs only current on the bus side, which H8 already accepted | **Costs carried:** diode drops (small at these currents), one small converter per domain, and the electrical isolation of the one-way link. The requirement is a *system-level* one of exactly the kind a YIL judge thinks in, and it is available as a public, checkable class figure rather than an invention | The achieved retention is now a derived quantity at P3 rather than an unexamined consequence, which makes the C43 cant trade three-way (C8 force ↔ M10 range ↔ C42 dissipation) instead of two-way. **Provenance note: the requirement is extracted from the fielded class, not chosen by the team** — it is recorded DEFINED on that basis and is the user's to re-take |

And the submission's two graceful-degradation sentences answer the **accuracy** question while a fuze-safety judge is asking the **ordnance-delivery** one | **Not a decision — a recorded gap, and the highest-value one in the submission.** Adopting buys close-support safety and costs the possibility of inhibiting a round that would have landed acceptably (risk 40); declining is defensible and silence is not. The mechanism is standard-supported either way (AOP-22 §3.6's safe-condition command mode and its safety-unacceptable-bit-pattern rejection criterion). The verified-source audit had this as *"✅ ours, and no fielded equivalent found"*; that verdict is reversed here, and the correction came from reading the standard rather than the articles | The claim joins the M1156 list — stated as a target we are meeting, never as an invention. **What survives as ours is narrower and must be stated precisely:** real-time re-synchronisation driven by *our own nav solution* rather than a ballistic/firing-table correction, and the bounded one-way gated form F1 already specifies. The **target sanity check stays ours** | None to the design. Carried as **risk 44**, owned by H11 |

It also gives **H6/N15 an external anchor** — §3.6(c) permits the fuze to calibrate its time base against the setter's precision time base | No value changes; the row's basis is externally specified rather than generic. **The window figures immediately expose risk 44**, which is the point of naming the standard |

E1 also gains the shock *shape* and duration as a stated requirement, per the gun-hard MEMS IMU specification |

The register's **gap list** now names the seven places where no source at Cite ≥ 3 exists — which is the form the anti-hallucination rule takes at the submission boundary | One more file to keep current. ## Data required post-selection

**This is not a live request and it is not a blocker.** YIL releases no technical data before shortlisting — that is the process, not a delay. Every criterion formerly listed here as `OPEN-BLOCKED` is now answered `PARAMETRIC` over the declared envelope in `design/02-reference-155mm-model.md`.

What follows is the **shopping list for P6**: the documents to obtain once the entry is shortlisted, what each one re-baselines, and what the design currently uses in its place. Until they arrive, the design proceeds on the interim basis shown.

| # | Document to obtain | Re-baselines | Interim basis in use today |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Shell drawing / datasheet — mass, CG, Ixx, Iyy, ogive contour | P1–P5, P8, P12, S1–S7 | Public M795 / M549A1 class + a geometry-calibrated mass-property model. **Expected re-baseline: the Sg calculation and the canard allowance (S1/S2) move first — this is the single largest post-selection change** |
| 2 | Fuze well drawing — thread, depth, sealing, torque spec | P6, P7, P9, P12, F9, C31 | MIL-STD-333B interface, thread 2″-12 UNS-2A, and the fielded M1156 PGK's 124.7 mm intrusion as the available depth |
| 3 | Firing tables — **state the range, and give the range-direction and deflection components separately** | M3, M4, M6, M7, M8 | Cited anchor: M549A1 CEP 267 m at 30.1 km → σ 227 m, bracketed by 259 m and 139 m citations. The range/deflection split is not public anywhere and remains the one genuine unknown in the sizing chain |
| 4 | Acceptable mass / CG / inertia change | P9, P10, P11 | Fielded-kit benchmark: ≤1.4 kg kit, ≤0.5 kg net of the fuze replaced; 2.6 mm net CG shift; +5.4 % Ixx |
| 5 | Gun and charge compatibility list | M3, M9, E1, E2 | Declared velocity envelope 546–943 m/s across 3M–6M, conservative against the public 3W–8S charge compatibility |
| 6 | Inductive fuze setter — in service or not, and its interface | F11, F12 | Class practice: inductive gun-line setting is the norm (the M1156 PGK is set with the Enhanced Portable Inductive Artillery Fuze Setter) |
| 7 | Terminal-effects / lethality data | M8, K4, R6 | r_eff = 30 m assumption, with 20 m and 50 m published as sensitivity |

**P6 exit condition (unchanged):** no criterion remains `PARAMETRIC` — every value traces either to a YIL document or to analysis performed on one.

## Open risk register

Design risks carried forward; each should be closed by a criterion or by analysis.

| # | Risk | Related criteria | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Friction versus Taero margin near apogee is the thinnest in the design. **C1 confirms where the margin comes from:** `Taero` is generated entirely by the control pair, so negative-direction ring authority is a *designed aerodynamic quantity*, not a by-product of the airframe. That makes the control pair's size a load-bearing design decision rather than a detail — and it is squeezed from both sides, since C10 requires `Taero > Tf` while the S1 stability allowance caps total canard area (risk 22) **C12 now closes the argument:** combining C10 at apogee with C9 at descent shows the control-pair sizing window **closes entirely at `Tf ≥ 0.1567 N·m`**, leaving only k ∈ (0.586, 0.892) at the measured 0.1 N·m. Friction is therefore a **feasibility gate on the architecture, not a preference to be minimised** | C1, C3, C9, C10, C12, C13, C22–C25, risk 22, risk 23 | Open — quantified; **friction is now a feasibility gate**; **preload ownership recorded 2026-09-18 — C23 sets one value against C35's `k ≥ 122 N/µm` and this budget, verified by C25 and on R7** |
| 2 | Ring-angle error is a direct, unattenuated error in guidance-force direction and is not self-correcting under IPP. **Now specified by G4/G6**: total ≈1.3° (1σ), split G6 ≤ 1.0° absolute and G5 ≤ 0.8° relative, giving 9.1 m of residual miss at the 80th-percentile correction. The reference alternator is rated **1.2 kW continuous, exceeded ×2.4**, and the bench rig ran body spin **21.6× slower**, so the same torque produced 21.6× less power and the problem was invisible. **This is the second instance of the rig's speed scaling hiding a full-scale failure mode** (the first being the apogee control loss). The energy cannot be stored — 121 kJ would need ≈1.5 F at 400 V, which will not fit the envelope or survive 15 000 g — so it must be dissipated as heat, in a nose that is already aerodynamically heated (W9, W10) and already over-subscribed for volume (risk 27). **Mitigation:** W7 must be sized against C42 rather than treated as a detail; the dump's mass, surface area and location must be resolved with the nose-packing budget; and the trade against a **servo-actuated canard architecture, which has no such load at all**, must be documented rather than assumed away | C42, C20, C43, W7, W9, W10, W1, risk 27, risk 24 | Open — new; **architectural, not a component issue.** **Extended 2026-09-18 (P6 check):** the interface the dump depends on is *not* delivered by the bare thread — flank + seat ≈ **3–16 W/K** against a **≥ 58 W/K** peak / **≥ 20–27 W/K** average requirement — so it must be designed (well-bore conduction path + a conductance-specified joint) and verified by test (P7/R7); and the 45 kg shell is a **long-time** sink (90 s penetration depth ≈ 30–65 mm), so the flight-time participating mass is the kit plus the shell's nose (≈ 6–8 kg, ≈ 25–35 K) and W7's 5.8 K is the asymptote |
| 33 | **The machine's mass is the dominant item in the kit and pushes the retrofit past the published benchmark.** Sizing gives **≈0.81–0.97 kg** for the machine alone (C40), against the fielded M1156's **1.4 kg for the entire kit** — and the M1156 achieves that with no alternator at all. The net penalty over the replaced ≈0.98 kg fuze moves from the reference model's **+0.42 kg (0.9 %)** toward **+0.7–0.9 kg**, with the CG shift moving from 2.6 mm to roughly **6–8 mm forward**. **Mitigation:** the shift is still only ≈0.8 % of projectile length and sits inside the platform's own 0.55–0.62 L envelope spread, so it is a soft failure, not a hard one. But S1 must check the **direction** of the stability effect — mass forward reduces the CP–CG separation, which reduces `C_Mα` and should *raise* `Sg`, the opposite of the usual intuition. C45 (make-versus-buy) and C44 (axial-flux trade) are the mass-reduction levers, and the W4 supercapacitor buffer adds a further ≈50–150 g in the same direction | C40, C21, S1, P9, P10, C44, C45, risk 20 | Open — new |
| 34 | **The ring-mounted IMU (N6) puts a new interface across the rotating joint: the ring's electronics need power in and data out, at up to 1912 rad/s of relative speed.** Two constraints make it non-trivial — it must be **contactless or near-contactless** (a slip ring would import friction straight into C13's feasibility-gated budget and adds brush wear across a 90 s flight plus shelf life), and it must fit the ring's occupied annulus inside risk 27's volume and C22's bearing envelope. What makes it tractable: **all flight power is generated body-side** (C34's coils are on the stator), so the ring carries only a sensor and its local conditioning, and **N9/N10 already measures relative rotation across this same joint** — the channel is shared, not new. **Mitigation:** fix the interface with N10 and C22 (rotary transformer for power, optical or capacitive for data) and verify it on the R7 rig, which runs exactly this joint. **Fallback, priced in N1:** if no contactless link fits, N6's option (b) — a body-fixed IMU with the spin handled in the filter — re-cuts the navigation line (nav 5 → 10 m → CEP 26.0 m, margin 13 %) | N6, N7, N8, N10, C13, C22, C26, P12, risk 4, risk 27, **H6** | Open — new; **carried by N6's decision, verified on R7. Extended 2026-09-19 (H6): the joint now also carries a *timing* obligation — the IMU's samples must be stamped on the ring or the link's delay measured and bounded, because the kit's time base lives body-side. H7 owns the fix.** **Design fixed 2026-09-19 (H7): rotary transformer for power, optical for data — the timestamp obligation is discharged by the optical path's bounded, measurable delay, so no ring-side clock is needed. Remaining exposure: fit inside C22's envelope and the ring's mass against C26, verified on the R7 rig, which runs this joint.** |
| 35 | **The W4 supercapacitor buffer is a new part class in the kit, and its survival of 15 000 g is not established.** A supercapacitor is attractive exactly where the design needs it — ≈200 J in ≈50–150 g at a low-voltage rail — but the common rolled construction is not rated anywhere near the launch environment (commercial parts are typically specified to tens of g, not thousands), and the cold end of E5/E6 raises ESR sharply, which is what the burst path depends on. **Mitigation:** make the buffer a **P4 selection with g-survival and temperature as the gate criteria** — potted cells, or a hybrid bank (supercapacitor for the slow duties, electrolytic/film for the fast burst edge, which is standard artillery practice). **The priced fallback is a potted electrolytic/film bank, which costs mass against P9** and sits inside risk 33's direction. **Verification: E4 (shock) and the R7 rig, which can impose a setback-representative pulse on a representative assembly.** Packing is a P4 item — preferentially body-side with the dump stage; if it must go in the fuze well it becomes a sixth claimant on risk 27's stack | W4, W5, W1–W3, P9, E4, E5, E6, R7, risk 27, risk 33 | Open — new; **qualification item, not a design blocker** |
| 36 | **The control loop's timing guarantee changed *kind* when a core replaced the fabric (H1).** G13 originally bought a *hardware* property: the loop could not be delayed by software at all, and it would have survived a fault in the application domain. The dual-core realisation keeps the substance — **two cores, two NVICs and independent clock dividers mean no software path runs from the propagation load to the loop**, and HRTIM → ADC → DMA means the *sampling instants* carry no software jitter either — but the guarantee is now a **bounded-WCET argument plus a measurement**, and the two cores share a PLL, a power domain and the bus matrix. **Three exposures, each addressed:** **(a) bus / SRAM contention** between the M7's propagation traffic and the M4's loop — the loop's code and data are confined to a RAM partition the guidance working set never touches (H2 — now a build-time property: a dedicated D2-domain SRAM partition the M4 image relocates into, since the M7's TCM is not addressable by the M4 at all), and the M4 executes from that partition rather than from flash; **(b) WCET estimation error** — the loop is ≈1 % duty at a 1 000 µs period, so even a 10× pessimism leaves ≈10× margin, and H4 carries the budget as a stated number rather than an assertion; **(c) the shared clock and power domain** — a fault there takes both cores, which the fabric would have absorbed. This one is *bounded rather than solved*: the safety-critical arming path is mechanical and the electronics only *enable* (F6), and the fuze's own function is autonomous (F1), so the failure direction is a dud and not a hazard. **Verification:** loop closure and jitter measured on the R7 rig, which already specifies closed-loop tracking with the DOB and the state-dependent limits. **Fallback priced, not hidden:** a flash-based FPGA can take the loop at P4 without moving the software boundary | H1, G13, G11, G12, H2, H4, R7, risk 19, W4 | Open — new; **a deliberate exchange whose price is stated rather than minimised** |
| 37 | **The ring-hold duty — and with it the 121 kJ — rests on a criterion inherited in its *generator-only* form, and that form is narrower than this design's own hardware.** C9's symmetric authority criterion (`Taero ≤ Talt_max(ω_rel)/2 + Tf`) and the C10/C12 feasibility gate come from the reference architecture, whose actuation is **brake-only** — a rectifier into a load bank, with the control pair's aerodynamic torque as the *only* torque source in the −X sense. This design is not brake-only: H8 specifies a four-quadrant power stage and W4 sizes the supercapacitor buffer for **motoring** bursts. If the machine motors as well as brakes, the net roll torque the control pair must generate becomes a **design variable rather than a constraint**, and C42's `P = Taero × ω_body` scales directly with it — a smaller net `Taero` means proportionally less heat, a smaller dump (W12) and less demanded of P6's thermal interface, with the ≈160 W of bearing friction (C13) and the machine's own losses remaining as the floor. **This is not a free lunch and it is not a change:** a smaller `Taero` moves the −X authority onto the machine and the buffer, where it is limited by *energy and current* rather than by torque; it re-cuts C9, C12 and C43; and it changes what C43's cant trade is buying. It is therefore recorded as a **P2 trade**, with the machine's four-quadrant capability (H8), the buffer's energy (W4) and the P6 interface requirement as its inputs — **the decided criteria stand unchanged for the submission.** **Corroboration that this term is easy to miss:** the predecessor study in this project holds a 0.2 N·m rotor torque at 16 000 rpm relative speed and books it as **2 W of copper loss** — the `T × ω` mechanical term (≈250–335 W) is absent from its ledger, the same class of omission the reference bench rig made by running body spin 21.6× slow (risk 24) | C1, C9, C10, C12, C13, C42, C43, H8, W4, W7, W12, P6, risk 24, risk 32 | Open — new 2026-09-19; **the decided criteria are unchanged; the trade is P2's** |
| 38 | **The P3 harness comes from a domain whose validity envelope excludes this flight regime.** RocketPy is validated on high-power and sounding rockets, and **its published validation table is two flights peaking at 86 m/s and 173 m/s** — 0.45 % and 0.75 % apogee error — against this design's **546–943 m/s [M3]** at **1107–1913 rad/s of spin [S6]**. Mechanically: its roll model uses the **small-spin approximation** (`v₀ ≫ ω·ξ`), its aerodynamic basis is **Barrowman-linear**, its body is **one rigid body with no ring DOF**, and it has **no in-bore phase**. It therefore cannot represent the de-spun ring, cannot compute our aerodynamics, and its demonstrated regime is a fraction of our slowest condition. **Two exposures.** **(a) Technical:** a harness result mistaken for a governing result would be wrong in ways this register cannot see — the same class of error the rig's speed scaling produced twice (risk 24) and the omission risk 37 records. **(b) Presentational:** an amateur-rocketry tool quoted as the trajectory authority in front of MoD/YIL is a credibility liability, and its own validation figures describe neither a gun launch, nor this velocity, nor a spinning airframe. **Mitigations, written into the decision rather than left to judgement:** harness, environment and cross-check **only**; **our own 7-DOF model governs every number that reaches the deck**; our coefficients enter through its coefficient-injection path, so it never computes our aerodynamics; its validation figures must never appear in the submission or in this register as evidence for this design's accuracy; and the governing validation remains published 155 mm range data, then YIL's firing tables at P6. **Verification:** P3's exit check includes a documented comparison of the two implementations, and **any criterion closing on a harness-only result is treated as still open** | S3, S5, S7, C11, N11, R5, R6, risk 8, risk 24, risk 37 | Open — **boundary decided and recorded; the exposure is misuse of the tool, not the tool** |
| 39 | **The anti-spoof answer is conditional on an external service, and the deck must not outrun it.** H11 selects authenticated GNSS (the H755's crypto engine, keys from the F12 package) — but the authentication *service* is the constellation's and the *support* is the receiver's, neither of which this project controls and both of which remain to be confirmed (F11's post-selection item, N2's constellation choice). The failure mode is claim integrity: if the service or the receiver cannot do it, "authenticated GNSS" in the submission becomes an overstatement of exactly the kind X5 was written to prevent for the seeker. **Mitigation:** the F11/N2 confirmation gates the claim, and the fallback is the B position — keys authenticate the setter only, the GNSS solution is unauthenticated, and the deck says so plainly. **Owners: H11 (the claim), N2/F11 (the confirmation), K5 (the part premium if it holds).** | H11, N2, N5, F11, K4, K5, risk 28 | Open — new; **conditional claim, fallback priced** |

| 40 | **The predicted-miss failsafe (F16) cuts both ways, and the design currently has no position on it.** The fielded kit in this class withholds detonation when it expects to impact more than 150 m from the loaded target — a close-support safety function, and the reason a battery can call precision fires near its own position. **Adopting it buys a real safety property and costs a real one:** the decision is made in flight on our own propagated prediction, so a prediction error can inhibit a round that would have landed acceptably, converting a usable near-miss into a dud. **Declining it is also defensible** — an unguided round of this class is a legitimate ballistic effect and the terminal-effect decision may belong to the user's doctrine — **but silence is not**: a fuze-safety judge will ask, the mechanism is public, and the submission's two graceful-degradation sentences answer the accuracy question while this one is about where a 43 kg HE round lands when the guidance has failed. **Mitigation:** F16 requires a stated position. Three forms are priced — adopt a threshold (radius, decision time and F6 arming-gate interaction all fixed); decline with a stated rationale; or adopt a **warn-only** form where the kit logs and reports the predicted miss but never withholds function, so the benefit accrues to the observer rather than the fuze. The mechanism is standard-supported either way: AOP-22 §3.6 defines a command mode that programs the fuze to a safe condition, and lists safety-unacceptable bit patterns among the fuze's own rejection criteria | **F16 (owner), F6, F1, F12, G1, R10, H13/R4**, AOP-22 §3.6 | **Open — new 2026-09-19; the highest-value gap in the submission.** Gates what slides 2 and 3 may claim **Closed 2026-09-20 by F16’s decision (option A): the position is adopted and recorded, so the exposure this risk named — a stated position being absent — is retired. What survives is the residual the risk itself predicted: a prediction error can dud a round that would have landed acceptably. It is bounded by taking the decision at the latest point the authority allows, and it is carried to F10 as a deliberate contribution to the dud rate rather than argued away.** |
| 41 | **Resonance instability is a named failure mode of this exact architecture, and the stability case does not name it.** A CFD/RBD virtual-flight platform for fixed-canard dual-spin projectiles *"reproduces the characteristic resonance instability of such projectiles"*, and complex-angle-of-attack theory gives the mechanism — canard disturbance resonating with the projectile's natural frequency, producing periodic velocity and acceleration fluctuation. **S1 is a gyroscopic criterion evaluated at the muzzle and does not answer an angular-motion criterion**; S3 and S5 bound the response to a *steady* disturbance, not a *periodic* one at the roll–coning frequency. **What makes this tractable rather than alarming:** the literature's own recommended fix is **reduced canard span with increased deflection angle**, which raises the pitching-moment derivative and the natural frequency — i.e. precisely C31's Ø151 mm span cap and C43's *cant angle, not area, is the lever*. The design has therefore already taken the disposition route; S8 asks for the evidence. **Mitigation:** S8, derived at P3 on the named vehicle alongside S3/S5; the failure must be demonstrated absent (or bounded) across the §8 envelope, not merely argued unlikely. Naming it also pre-empts the question a domain judge will ask by name | **S8 (owner), S1, S2, S3, S5, C4/C5, C31, C43** — Defence Technology 2025; Acta Armamentarii 2026 (DOI 10.12382/bgxb.2026.0534); Norris et al., Defence Technology 16(1) 2020; Zhu 2014 | Open — new 2026-09-19; **disposition scheduled at P3** |
| 42 | **HERO / EMR / ESD are absent from the qualification set.** E10's list stops at MIL-STD-810 / 461 / 1316 and E11 covers insensitive munitions — neither covers ordnance electromagnetic hazards. **This kit carries the fuze's own exposures plus one the fielded fuze does not:** a **24 GHz emitter in the nose** (F2), a fireset with an out-of-line rotor (F6), and a machine whose magnets and switching converter are a designed-in magnetic source (F15). A HERO assessment is also a *system* property — round plus platform plus magazine — so it reaches beyond the kit into how the round is stowed and handled. **Mitigation:** E12, with the standard set named at P4 (MIL-HDBK-240 / MIL-STD-464 class for HERO and EME, STANAG 4234 class for EMR, MIL-STD-1686 / STANAG 4497 class for ESD), editions verified against YIL's qualification baseline post-selection, and the assessment run jointly with P12's well stack and F6's arming logic. **Why it is worth a row rather than a line:** the pair F6 + E12 is a clean argument — nothing *initiates* it without two independent stimuli, and nothing can *make* it initiate from outside | **E12 (owner), E10, E11, F2, F6, F15, P12** | Open — new 2026-09-19; **standards named at P4, assessment at P7** |
| 43 | **Range retention is unbounded — nothing prices what the kit costs in range.** The kit is specified as a drop-in retrofit that must not change the shell, charge system or gun, so any range it costs, it costs through the canards' drag and the kit's own 1.4–1.7 kg. **Until M10 there was no criterion owning that exchange rate**, which meant canard sizing (C4/C5) and cant angle (C43) could buy accuracy at the range's expense with nothing checking the trade. The fielded kit's own published claim sets the bar — *"maintains >90 % of range capability of conventional all up round"* — and it is the kind of system-level requirement a YIL judge thinks in. **Mitigation:** M10 DEFINED at the >90 % class figure; the achieved retention is derived at P3 alongside the drag build-up, and any correction that costs more than 10 % of the range must then be justified against M10 rather than against C8 alone. **Interaction worth watching:** M10 and C43 pull in the same direction (less cant, less drag) while C8 pulls the other way — which makes this a three-way trade rather than a two-way one, and it is currently unrecorded in the C43 row | **M10 (owner), C4, C5, C8, C43, M7** — Orbital ATK NDIA 2015 | Open — new 2026-09-19; **retention derived at P3** |
| 44 | **The setter mission package (F12) has never been counted against the standard's own window.** F12 claims target coordinates, met, charge/muzzle-velocity state, time reference, **GNSS ephemeris and keys**, mode and parameters, and round ID — with a read-back — through the in-service inductive setter. AOP-22 fixes the physically available capacity: the forward message window is **275 ± 5 ms at 1 000 ± 10 bit/s**, i.e. ≈275 bits per window, and the two **registered course-correction fuzes in Annex E (XM7, XM8) use two windows** — so ≈550 bits is the standard's own budget for a whole CCF mission package. **GNSS ephemeris alone is of that order**, and keys, coordinates, met and the read-back all have to fit inside it. F12 lists the contents and F11 defers capacity confirmation to post-selection, which is the right instinct but leaves the arithmetic undone. **Mitigation:** H11 owns the count — budget the package in bits against the AOP-22 window, split it across the two windows the standard already provides for a course-correction fuze, and cut or defer whatever does not fit (ephemeris can be aged/almanac-only; keys can be shorter-lived). F12's fallback to split programming (option B) is priced but should not be the first answer. **This is cheap to close and expensive to be asked** — a setter engineer will know the window | **H11 (owner), F11, F12, N3, H10, H11's reserve-domain front end**, AOP-22 §3 and Annex E | Open — new 2026-09-19; **the bit budget is a short derivation, not a design change** |
| 45 | **Thermal-preload runaway across the bearing pair is unowned.** C23 deliberately buys air-gap stiffness — the 122 N/µm radial case from C35 — with **preload**, and the ring runs at up to ≈1 900 rad/s relative. At that speed the friction torque the preload creates is spent as heat (`Q ∝ M_friction × ω`, so C13's "flat" 0.10 N·m budget is already ≈160 W), and the established high-speed failure path is a closed loop: **friction → heat → clearance loss → preload rise → friction rise**. A preload that is correct cold and at low speed can be excessive once the rings are at operating temperature. **Our geometry makes it sharper than usual:** the air gap is 0.40 mm with a 0.20 mm maximum relative deflection allowance, so the clearance that the loop consumes is exactly the clearance C35 is protecting. **Mitigation:** extend the C23 preload trade to be **purpose-driven and thermally bounded** — specify preload against the stiffness requirement only, state the operating-clearance budget at temperature, choose the fit and the lubricant quantity for it, and verify the pair's torque-versus-temperature behaviour on the R7 rig (which already runs the joint at speed and can measure both torque and housing temperature). Also note E8's companion findings: contact seals are a large and avoidable friction share (so open or shielded), grease *quantity* is first-order in a miniature bearing, and cold-start torque rises — which is the cold corner of R5's campaign. **Corroboration that this class of term is easy to miss:** risk 24 (the reference rig's 19.4× speed scaling) and risk 37 (the reference's absent `T × ω` term) are both omissions of exactly this kind | **C23 (owner), C13, C22, C25, C35, W12, R7, E5/E6/E7** | Open — new 2026-09-19; **owned by C23, verified on R7** |
| 46 | **C42's published 2.92 kW peak does not reconcile with the hold torque the rest of the register carries — and C42 is the number W7, W12 and P6 are sized on.** C42 states *"`P = Taero × ω_body` peaks at 2.92 kW at launch"*. At the launch spin of 1 623 rad/s that implies `Taero` ≈ **1.80 N·m**. But C11 and the machine sizing carry a hold duty of **2.36 N·m** (`Taero_max` 2.2 + `Tf` 0.157), which at the same speed gives **3.83 kW** — 31 % higher. The sizing document's own §1 duty table and the visualisation's readouts both use the 2.36 N·m figure. **Two readings are possible and the register does not currently say which is intended:** either the duty torque is referenced to a *different point in the spin decay* (2.2 N·m × the apogee spin of ≈1 352 rad/s ≈ 2.97 kW, which lands on 2.92 kW within rounding), or one of the two figures is simply wrong. **Why this matters more than a bookkeeping slip:** the ≥ 58 W/K peak interface requirement (W7/P6) and the dump stage (W12) are both derived from C42, so if the governing peak is 3.8 kW rather than 2.9 kW the hard thermal problem grows by roughly a third — and P6's bare-joint shortfall (≈3–16 W/K against the requirement) is the design's thinnest margin. **Mitigation:** state the *reference speed* on C42 explicitly and reconcile the duty table against it at **P2**, under the same trade that risk 37 already opens; until then, **no submission artefact may quote the visualisation's on-screen figure as C42's value** (the visualisation now carries this note). Owner: C42, with W7/W12/P6 as the affected rows | **C42 (owner), C11, C13, C20, C43, W7, W12, P6, R7** | Open — new 2026-09-19; **found while reconciling the visualisation against the register.** A reference-speed statement is a one-line fix; the thermal consequence may not be |
